United States [pride flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_flag)

[^cite_note-1]

[^cite_note-2]

[Lesbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesbian), [gay](5171128.md), [bisexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisexual), [transgender](19904525.md), and [queer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queer) ([LGBTQ](78174325.md)) rights in the [United States](3434750.md) have developed over time, with [public opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_of_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) and [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurisprudence) changing significantly since the late 1980s. Lesbian, gay and bisexual rights are considered advanced,[^cite_note-3][^cite_note-4][^cite_note-SS-5][^cite_note-PO-6][^cite_note-7] but rights of transgender people have faced significant erosion since the beginning of [Donald Trump's second presidency](78298108.md).[^cite_note-8]

In 1962, beginning with Illinois, states began to decriminalize same-sex sexual activity,[^cite_note-9] and in 2003, through *[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)*, all remaining laws against same-sex sexual activity were invalidated. In 2004, beginning with Massachusetts, states began to offer same-sex marriage, and in 2015, through *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*, all states were required to offer it. In many states and municipalities, LGBTQ Americans are explicitly protected from discrimination in employment, housing, and access to public accommodations. Many LGBTQ rights in the United States have been established by the [United States Supreme Court](31737.md), which [invalidated state laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romer_v._Evans) banning protected class recognition based upon [homosexuality](5488304.md), [struck down sodomy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas) nationwide, [struck down Section 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor) of the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), made [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges) legal nationwide, and [prohibited employment discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County) against gay and transgender employees. [LGBTQ-related](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights) anti-discrimination laws regarding housing and private and public services vary by state. Twenty-three states plus [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_the_District_of_Columbia#Discrimination_protections), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Guam), and [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Puerto_Rico#Discrimination_protections) outlaw discrimination based on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation), and twenty-two states plus Washington, D.C., outlaw discrimination based on [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) or expression.[^cite_note-10] Family law also varies by state. [Adoption of children by same-sex married couples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_adoption_in_the_United_States) is legal nationwide since *Obergefell v. Hodges*.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] According to [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign)'s 2024 state index, the states with the most comprehensive LGBTQ rights legislation include [Vermont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Vermont), [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_California), [Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Minnesota), [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Virginia), [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Massachusetts), [Rhode Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Rhode_Island), [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Maryland), [New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_New_Mexico), [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Washington_\(state\)), [Colorado](30333291.md), [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_New_York), [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Illinois), [Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Oregon), [Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Maine), [Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Hawaii), and [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_New_Jersey).[^cite_note-13]

Hate crimes based on sexual orientation or gender identity are punishable by federal law under the [Matthew Shepard and James Byrd, Jr. Hate Crimes Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Shepard_and_James_Byrd,_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Prevention_Act), which provides funding and technical assistance to jurisdictions to help more effectively investigate hate crimes, but many states lack laws that cover sexual orientation and/or gender identity.[^cite_note-14]

[Public opinion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_of_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) is overwhelmingly supportive of same-sex marriage and it is no longer considered a significant topic of public debate.[^cite_note-SS-5] A 2022 *Grinnell College National Poll* found that 74% of Americans agree that same-sex marriage should be a guaranteed right while 13% disagree.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16] According to the [General Social Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Social_Survey), support for same-sex marriage among 18–34 year olds is near-universal.[^cite_note-RM-17]

Public opinion on transgender issues is divided. Top issues regarding [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) include [bathroom access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathroom_bill), [athletics](46676322.md), and [transgender-related healthcare for minors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights_in_the_United_States#Healthcare).[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

After transgender people faced significant erosions in rights on the state level in Republican ran states over the course of three years,[^cite_note-21] President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) issued an [executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defending_Women_from_Gender_Ideology_Extremism_and_Restoring_Biological_Truth_to_the_Federal_Government) on January 20, 2025, directing the United States government to remove all federal protections for transgender individuals and remove all recognition of transgender identity. The order declared that only male and female genders are recognized, and states that official documents must reflect biological sex (either male or female) assigned at birth. Previously, it was possible for US passport holders to receive either gender marker, or an "X" marker, simply by declaration during a passport application. Trump also banned trans people from military service and halted financing to [gender-affirming care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender-affirming_care) for individuals younger than 19.[^cite_note-22] References to transgender people were scrubbed from government websites, in some cases by using the acronym "[LGB](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/LGB)." Over 350 pages about the LGBTQ community at large were removed entirely.[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24]

## Public opinion

Main article: [Public opinion of same-sex marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_of_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States)



Two women at the Capital [Pride Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_Parade), Washington, DC (2014)

[Public opinion of same-sex marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_opinion_of_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) has changed significantly since the late 1980s;[^cite_note-PO-6] in the early 2020s, an overwhelming majority of [Americans](19792942.md) approved of the legality of these marriages,[^cite_note-PO-6] with younger people more likely to express support.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-RM-17]

From 1988 to 2009, support for recognized same-sex marriage increased between 1% and 1.5% per year,[^cite_note-26] and accelerated thereafter, rising above 50% in *[Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center)* polling for the first time in 2011.[^cite_note-Changing_Attitudes_on_Gay_Marriage-27] Furthermore, a 2012 Gallup poll found 61% support for allowing gay and lesbian individuals to adopt children.[^cite_note-28]

"The transformation of America's response to homosexuality", Jeremiah Garretson wrote in 2018, "has been — and continues to be — one of the most rapid and sustained shifts in mass attitudes since the start of public polling."[^cite_note-PO-6]

A 2021 *[Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute)* poll about legal recognition of same-sex marriage found majority support (defined as at least 50% support) in 47 states, ranging from 50% in [South Carolina](27956.md) to 85% in [Massachusetts](1645518.md). A 48th state, [Alabama](303.md), had plurality support (defined as more supporters than opponents, with neither side reaching 50%). Only [Arkansas](1930.md) and [Mississippi](16949861.md) had majority opposition. When PRRI repeated the poll in 2022 and 2023, no state had majority opposition.[^cite_note-29] A 2022 *[Quinnipiac University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinnipiac_University)* poll found 68% support nationwide.[^cite_note-30] Gallup's 2022 and 2023 nationwide polls found 71% support; however, in 2024, this support decreased to 69%.[^cite_note-31]

In 2024, the PRRI found that the support for same-sex marriage and for LGBTQ discrimination protections in US states is inversely correlated with the support for [Christian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_nationalism).[^cite_note-32]

Two 2025 Gallup polls indicated that support for LGBTQ+ rights was polarizing among party lines, with the first poll finding that 88% of Democrats and 76% of independents are in support of same-sex marriage, but only 41% of Republicans in support of same-sex marriage. The first poll also found that 86% of Democrats and 69% of independents saw same-sex relationships as morally acceptable, compared to only 38% of Republicans.[^cite_note-33]

The second poll found that 69% of Americans, 90% of Republicans, 72% of independents and 41% of Democrats, supported laws that required transgender athletes to participate in sports aligned with their assigned sex at birth. 66% of Americans, 89% of Republicans, 66% of independents and 38% of Democrats, supported laws that required assigned sex at birth to be listed on government documents such as drivers licenses and passports. 40% of Americans, 70% of Democrats, 45% of independents and only 9% of Republicans, believe that gender transition is morally acceptable.[^cite_note-34]

## Legality of same-sex sexual activity

Main article: [Sodomy laws in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy_laws_in_the_United_States)

See also: [Crime against nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_against_nature) and [Ages of consent in North America § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ages_of_consent_in_North_America#United_States)

&#x20; 1977

&#x20; 2003

On June 26, 2003, the Supreme Court ruled in *[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)* that intimate consensual sexual conduct is part of the liberty protected by substantive [due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process) under the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md). The majority opinion, written by Justice [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), explicitly overruled *Bowers v. Hardwick,* a 1986 decision that found sodomy laws to be constitutional.[^cite_note-OC-35] Despite this ruling, some states have not repealed their sodomy laws[^cite_note-sodus-36] and local law enforcement officers have used these statutes to harass or arrest gay people.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-rnodrop-38][^cite_note-majones11-39] After the repeal of "[Don't Ask Don't Tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask_Don't_Tell)" in 2011, the U.S. Congress repealed sodomy laws in the U.S. military in 2014.

Prior to the 2003 Supreme Court ruling in *Lawrence v. Texas,* same-sex sexual activity was illegal in 14 U.S. states, Puerto Rico, and the U.S. military. By that time, 29 states, the District of Columbia, and five territories had repealed their state's sodomy laws by legislative action.[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-ILGA_2014-41] Twelve states have had state Supreme Court or state Appeals courts rule that their state's sodomy laws were unconstitutional. Georgia, Louisiana, and Massachusetts, have all had their state sodomy laws struck down by the courts, but the legislatures have not repealed those laws.[^cite_note-42] On April 18, 2013, the governor of Montana signed a bill repealing that state's sodomy law; it had previously been nullified by the Montana Supreme Court.[^cite_note-43] On April 23, 2014, the governor of Virginia signed a bill repealing that state's sodomy law.[^cite_note-44] Utah and Alabama repealed their sodomy laws in 2019 and Idaho did in 2022. On October 1, 2020, a bill repealing Maryland's sodomy law went into effect without the governor's signature,[^cite_note-45] and a bill repealing its "unnatural sex practices" law went into effect without the governor's signature in May 2023.[^cite_note-46] Minnesota also repealed its sodomy law in 2023.[^cite_note-47]

12 states either have not yet formally repealed their laws against sexual activity among consenting adults or have not revised them to accurately reflect their true scope in the aftermath of *Lawrence v. Texas*. Often, the sodomy law was drafted to also encompass other forms of sexual conduct such as bestiality, and no attempt has subsequently succeeded in separating them. 9 states' statutes purport to ban all forms of sodomy, some including oral intercourse, regardless of the participants' genders: [Florida](18933066.md), [Georgia](48830.md), [Louisiana](18130.md), [Massachusetts](1645518.md), [Michigan](18859.md), [Mississippi](16949861.md), [North Carolina](21650.md), [Oklahoma](22489.md) and [South Carolina](27956.md). 3 states specifically target their statutes at same-sex relations only: [Kansas](16716.md), [Kentucky](16846.md), and [Texas](29810.md).

The [age of consent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ages_of_consent_in_the_United_States) in each jurisdiction varies but, in most jurisdictions, it is equal to the age of consent for heterosexual sex.[^cite_note-48] The exception to this is Texas, whose statute books still hold an outdated [Romeo and Juliet law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romeo_and_Juliet_law) that makes the age of consent for gay and lesbian teenagers unequal to that for heterosexual ones.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] Researchers have shown that sodomy law repeals led to a decline in the number of arrests for disorderly conduct, prostitution, and other sex offenses, as well as a reduction in arrests for drug and alcohol consumption, in line with the hypothesis that sodomy law repeals enhanced mental health and lessened minority stress.[^cite_note-51]

## Recognition of marriage and adoption for same-sex couples

See also: [Same-sex marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States), [Timeline of same-sex marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States), [Same-sex unions in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_unions_in_the_United_States), [Domestic partnership in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_partnership_in_the_United_States), [Cohabitation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohabitation_in_the_United_States), and [Common-law marriage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common-law_marriage_in_the_United_States)

### Marriage



2011 protest in [New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_New_Jersey) by [Garden State Equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_State_Equality) in support of same-sex marriage rights and against [deportation](412990.md) of LGBTQ spouses.

The movement to obtain [civil marriage rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights_and_responsibilities_of_marriages_in_the_United_States) and benefits for same-sex couples in the United States began in the 1970s but remained unsuccessful for over 40 years. On May 17, 2004, [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Massachusetts) became the first U.S. state and the sixth jurisdiction in the world to legalize same-sex marriage following the [Supreme Judicial Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Supreme_Judicial_Court)'s decision six months earlier.[^cite_note-festive-52] Before nationwide legalization, same-sex marriage became legal in 36 states: 24 states by court order, nine by legislative action, and three by [referendum](51582.md). Some states had legalized same-sex marriage by more than one of the three actions.

On June 26, 2015, the Supreme Court ruled in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)* that states must license and recognize same-sex marriages. Consequently, same-sex marriage is legal in all 50 states, [the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_District_of_Columbia), [Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Puerto_Rico), [Guam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Guam), [U.S. Virgin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_U.S._Virgin_Islands), and [Northern Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Northern_Mariana_Islands). Currently, same-sex marriages are recognized in [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_American_Samoa), due to the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act). The legal status of same-sex marriage also varies in [Native American tribal nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_tribal_nations_in_the_United_States), as their reservations are considered sovereign entities and were not affected by the Supreme Court's legalization in 2015.

### Civil unions

Main article: [List of U.S. state laws on same-sex unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_laws_on_same-sex_unions)

Prior to nationwide same-sex marriage, 15 U.S. states had civil unions or domestic partnerships. The first state to allow same-sex unions was [Vermont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Vermont) in July 2000.[^cite_note-53] Ten of these states retain those laws as a continued choice for same-sex couples and opposite-sex couples.

### Adoption

Main article: [Same-sex adoption in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_adoption_in_the_United_States)

Same-sex couples are allowed to adopt in states and territories following the ruling in [Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges) legalizing same-sex marriage. Prior to *Obergefell*, various states by legislative and judicial action had allowed joint adoption by same-sex couples.

### Citizenship

[Naturalized U.S. citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalized_U.S._citizen) whose biological children are born abroad may be unable to obtain U.S. citizenship for their children even if their spouse is also a U.S. citizen. This may disproportionately affect same-sex couples, given that typically only one spouse is biologically related to the child.[^cite_note-Mervosh-54] In October 2020, with representation by [Lambda Legal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambda_Legal), [Immigration Equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Equality_\(organization\)) and law firm [Morgan Lewis & Bockius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Lewis_&_Bockius);[^cite_note-Greene-55] the [United States Department of State](31975.md) withdrew its appeal of the verdict in *Kiviti v. Pompeo*, and declined to appeal *Mize-Gregg v. Pompeo*.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-Mervosh-54] The State Department's refusal to recognize children born overseas to married same-sex, American citizen couples as U.S. citizens was ruled to be unlawful by Federal district court judges in both cases.[^cite_note-Greene-55][^cite_note-57]

### Former restrictions

Defense of Marriage Act

Main article: [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act)

See also: [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act)

The United States Congress enacted the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) (DOMA) in 1996, which forbade the federal government from recognizing same-sex marriages and relieved states of the requirement that they recognize same-sex unions performed in other jurisdictions. On June 26, 2013, Section 3 of DOMA ("Definition of marriage") was ruled unconstitutional by the U.S. Supreme Court in *[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)*. The law became effectively unenforceable after the U.S. Supreme Court decision *Obergefell v. Hodges* (2015) and was fully repealed by the [Respect for Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respect_for_Marriage_Act) in 2022.

Former state bans on same-sex marriage

See also: [U.S. state constitutional amendments banning same-sex unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._state_constitutional_amendments_banning_same-sex_unions), [List of U.S. state constitutional amendments banning same-sex unions by type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._state_constitutional_amendments_banning_same-sex_unions_by_type), and [Same-sex marriage legislation in the United States § Efforts to ban same-sex unions by constitutional amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_legislation_in_the_United_States#Efforts_to_ban_same-sex_unions_by_constitutional_amendment)

After the passage of the DOMA in 1996, many state legislators enacted state statutes, nicknamed mini-DOMA's, that ban same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-58] After Massachusetts legalized same-sex marriage in 2004, 14 states amended their constitution to ban recognition of same-sex marriages and many banning civil unions as well. 28 states passed state constitutional amendments that banned same-sex marriage: [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_Sanctity_of_Marriage_Amendment), [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Ballot_Measure_2_\(1998\)), [Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona_Proposition_102_\(2008\)), [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Proposition_8), [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Amendment_43_\(2006\)), [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Florida_Amendment_2), [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_Constitutional_Amendment_1_\(2004\)), [Idaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idaho_Amendment_2_\(2006\)), [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_proposed_amendment_1_\(2005\)), [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Constitutional_Amendment_1_\(2004\)), [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Constitutional_Amendment_1_\(2004\)), [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_State_Proposal_%E2%80%93_04-2_\(2004\)), [Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Amendment_1_\(2004\)), [Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Constitutional_Amendment_2_\(2004\)), [Montana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montana_Initiative_96_\(2004\)), [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebraska_Initiative_Measure_416_\(2000\)), [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nevada_Question_2_\(2002\)), [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Amendment_1), [North Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Dakota_Constitutional_Measure_1_\(2004\)), [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_Issue_1_\(2004\)), [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_State_Question_711_\(2004\)), [Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oregon_Ballot_Measure_36_\(2004\)), [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Carolina_Amendment_1_\(2006\)), [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota_Amendment_C_\(2006\)), [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Amendment_1_\(2006\)), [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_proposition_2_\(2005\)), [Utah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utah_Constitutional_Amendment_3), [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall-Newman_Amendment), and [Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_Referendum_1_\(2006\)).

Hawaii voters approved a narrower [constitutional amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Constitutional_Amendment_2_\(1998\)) empowering the legislature to outlaw same-sex marriage, which they had already done in 1993. On November 6, 2012, [Minnesota](19590.md) became the first state to vote down a [proposed constitutional amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Amendment_1) banning same-sex marriage. The amendment failed with a 53% to 47% vote.[^cite_note-59] All state constitutional and statutory bans on same-sex marriage were declared unconstitutional in June 2015 in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*.

## Discrimination protections



Map of states, counties, and municipalities that have sexual orientation and gender identity discrimination prohibited in employment, housing, and public accommodations via statute, executive order, regulation, and/or court ruling:\
&#x20;

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** and **gender identity** discrimination prohibited in employment, housing, and public accommodations

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** and **gender identity** discrimination prohibited in employment and public accommodations, but not housing

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** and **gender identity** discrimination prohibited in employment and housing, but not public accommodations

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** discrimination prohibited in employment, housing, and public accommodations, while *gender identity* discrimination prohibited in only employment and housing

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** discrimination prohibited in employment, housing, and public accommodations, while *gender identity* discrimination prohibited in only employment

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** discrimination prohibited in employment and housing, while *gender identity* discrimination prohibited in only employment

&#x20; **Sexual orientation** discrimination prohibited in employment and public accommodations, while *gender identity* discrimination prohibited in only employment

&#x20; *Sexual orientation* and *gender identity* discrimination prohibited in employment since *[Bostock v. Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County)*

---

&#x20; State has law which prohibits local discrimination protections for *sexual orientation* or *gender identity* in housing or public accommodations

The [Equality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_\(United_States\)), which had been introduced in the [United States Congress](31756.md) in February 2021, would have outlawed [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination) based on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) and [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) nationwide.[^cite_note-equality_act-60] However, shortly after his inauguration in 2025, [Donald Trump](4848272.md) signed an [executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defending_Women_from_Gender_Ideology_Extremism_and_Restoring_Biological_Truth_to_the_Federal_Government) ordering the non-enforcement discrimination laws for gender identity and sexual orientation.[^cite_note-61] This is despite the Supreme Court ruling in [Bostock v Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County) that stated discrimination of this kind fell under Title VII of the [1964 Civil Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), which outlawed discrimination based on someone's sex.[^cite_note-62]

### Anti-discrimination laws

U.S. federal law does not explicitly include protections against [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination) on the basis of [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) or [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity). In June 2020, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled that sexual orientation and gender identity are included under "sex" as a prohibited ground of employment discrimination in the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964).[^cite_note-JR-63] The ruling may impact other federal civil rights barring sex discrimination in education, health care, housing, and financial credit.

Explicit and comprehensive anti-discrimination protections based on sexual orientation and gender identity have been proposed by the [United States Congress](31756.md) under the [Equality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_\(United_States\)), which was passed in the [House](19468510.md) by a vote of 236–173 on May 17, 2019,[^cite_note-equality_act-60][^cite_note-64] but stalled in the [Senate](24909346.md).[^cite_note-65] The bill was reintroduced by the [119th Congress](70645797.md) on April 29, 2025 in both the House of Representatives and in the Senate.[^cite_note-66]

During the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md), [The Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation), with contributors from the [cabinet of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cabinet_of_Donald_Trump), outlined legislation on [Project 2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_2025) for a large rollback of LGBTQ rights in the United States, a rollback of [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_the_United_States) in the US, as well as a rollback on all legal protections on the basis of "gender identity."[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69]

#### Employment



Map of states that have **sexual orientation** and **gender identity** discrimination prohibited in public and/or private employment via state statute, executive order, regulation, and/or case law. Note: Employment discrimination based on sexual orientation or gender identity is also prohibited under federal law.\
&#x20;

&#x20; Sexual orientation and gender identity discrimination prohibited in public employment

&#x20; Gender identity discrimination prohibited in public employment

&#x20; Sexual orientation discrimination prohibited in public employment.

&#x20; Sexual orientation and gender identity discrimination prohibited in public employment.

&#x20; Sexual orientation and gender identity discrimination prohibited in public employment only

&#x20; No state-level prohibition on discrimination based on sexual orientation or gender identity

&#x20; Sexual orientation discrimination prohibited in public employment only

Main article: [LGBT employment discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_employment_discrimination_in_the_United_States)

[Employment discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_discrimination) refers to discriminatory employment practices such as bias in hiring, promotion, job assignment, termination, and compensation, and various types of harassment.[^cite_note-70]

There is no federal statute explicitly addressing employment discrimination based on sexual orientation or gender identity. However, in June 2020, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled that sexual orientation and gender identity are included under "sex" as a prohibited ground of employment discrimination in the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964).[^cite_note-JR-63] This effectively means that in the U.S., no employer can fire an employee on the basis of sexual orientation or gender identity.

24 states, the [District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), [Guam](11974.md), and over 140 cities and counties have enacted bans on discrimination based on sexual orientation and/or sexual identity. Additionally, some states have laws or regulations that ban discrimination based on gender identity and/or sexual orientation in public employment only.

Presidents have also established certain protections for some employees of the federal government by executive order. In 1995, President [Bill Clinton](3356.md)'s [Executive Order 12968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_12968) establishing criteria for the issuance of security clearances included sexual orientation for the first time in its non-discrimination language: "The United States Government does not discriminate based on race, color, religion, sex, national origin, disability, or sexual orientation in granting access to classified information." It also said that "no inference" about suitability for access to classified information "may be raised solely on the basis of the sexual orientation of the employee."[^cite_note-71] Clinton's [Executive Order 13087](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13087) in 1998 prohibited discrimination based on sexual orientation in the competitive service of the federal civilian workforce. It applied to the large majority of federal employees, but not to the [excepted services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excepted_service) such as the military.[^cite_note-72]

At the start of 2010, the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) included gender identity among the classes protected against discrimination under the authority of the [Equal Employment Opportunity Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Employment_Opportunity_Commission) (EEOC). In 2012, the EEOC ruled that Title VII of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) does not allow gender identity-based employment discrimination because it is a form of sexual discrimination.[^cite_note-Edgeboston.com-73][^cite_note-Carpenter-74][^cite_note-Tatectate-75]

On July 21, 2014, Obama signed [Executive Order 13672](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13672), adding "gender identity" to the categories protected against discrimination in hiring in the federal civilian workforce, and both "sexual orientation" and "gender identity" to the categories protected against discrimination in hiring and employment on the part of federal government contractors and sub-contractors.[^cite_note-The_White_House-76][^cite_note-77] Obama's related Executive Order 13673[^cite_note-78] required federal contractors to prove their compliance with [labor laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_law_in_the_United_States), but Trump revoked this requirement on March 27, 2017.[^cite_note-Vogue-79]

As of June 15, 2020, all persons working for public employers that employ more than 15 people are protected from discrimination based solely on the grounds of sexual orientation or gender identity via the Supreme Court's landmark decision in [Bostock v. Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County).[^cite_note-80] The plaintiffs of the case include [Gerald Bostock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Lynn_Bostock), [Aimee Stephens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aimee_Stephens), and Donald Zarda, each fired based upon their sexuality or gender identity. Two-thirds of registered U.S. voters agree with the Supreme Court's decision that employment nondiscrimination laws should protect LGBTQ identity, according to a HuffPost/YouGov poll conducted several days later.[^cite_note-81]

#### Housing



States that prohibit housing discrimination based on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) or [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity). [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development) regulations require all housing providers that receive HUD funding not to discriminate against an individual's sexual orientation or gender identity.&#x20;

&#x20; Prohibits housing discrimination based on sexual orientation and gender identity

&#x20; Prohibits housing discrimination based on sexual orientation only

&#x20; Does not factor sexual orientation or gender identity/unclear

Main article: [Office of Fair Housing and Equal Opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Fair_Housing_and_Equal_Opportunity)

Further information: [Housing discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_discrimination_in_the_United_States)

The **Office of Fair Housing and Equal Opportunity** (**FHEO**) is an agency within the [United States Department of Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development). FHEO is responsible for administering and enforcing federal fair housing laws and establishing policies that make sure all Americans have equal access to the housing of their choice. Housing discrimination refers to discrimination against potential or current tenants by landlords. In the United States, there is no federal law against such discrimination based on sexual orientation or gender identity, but at least twenty-two states and many major cities have enacted laws prohibiting it.[^cite_note-82] See, for example, [Washington House Bill 2661](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_House_Bill_2661).

In 2012, the United States Department of Housing and Urban Development's [Office of Fair Housing and Equal Opportunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Fair_Housing_and_Equal_Opportunity) issued a regulation called "Equal Access" to prohibit LGBTQ discrimination in federally-assisted housing programs.[^cite_note-83] It ensures that the Department's core housing programs are open to all eligible persons, regardless of sexual orientation or gender identity. In 2019, however, there was an attempt to weaken the regulation.[^cite_note-RI-84]

The Office of Fair Housing and Equal Opportunity is responsible for enforcing a variety of fair housing laws, which prohibit discrimination in both privately owned and publicly assisted housing including:

- The [Fair Housing Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Housing_Act)

- Title VI of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964)

- Section 504 of the [Rehabilitation Act of 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehabilitation_Act_of_1973)

- Section 109 of Title I of the [Housing and Community Development Act of 1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_and_Community_Development_Act_of_1974)

- Title II of the [Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_with_Disabilities_Act_of_1990)

- [Architectural Barriers Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_Barriers_Act_of_1968)

- [Age Discrimination Act of 1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_Discrimination_Act_of_1975)

- [Title IX of the Education Amendments Act of 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX)

Among the landmark civil cases on gay rights in housing is [Braschi v Stahl Associates Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braschi_v._Stahl_Associates_Co.). In 1989 New York Court of Appeals case that decided that plaintiff Miguel Braschi, the surviving partner of a same-sex relationship, counted as "family" under New York law and was thus able to continue living in a rent controlled apartment belonging to the deceased partner.[^cite_note-casebriefs.com-85][^cite_note-86]

#### Medical facilities

On April 14, 2010, President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) issued an [Executive Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_order_\(United_States\)) to the [Department of Health and Human Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services) to draft new rules for all hospitals accepting [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) or [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) funds. They would require facilities to grant visitation and medical decision-making rights to gay and lesbian partners, as well as designees of others such as widows and widowers.[^cite_note-nytimesobama-87] Such rights are not protected by law in many states. Obama said he was inspired by the case of a Florida family, where one of the mothers died while her partner and four children were denied visitation by the hospital.[^cite_note-nytimesobama-87] On June 12, 2020, the Trump administration issued a new rule stating that sexual orientation and gender identity were not covered under the anti-discrimination protections of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act),[^cite_note-88] but this was reversed by the Biden administration, restoring the Obama-era policy.[^cite_note-Alonso-Zaldivar-89]

According to the American advocacy group [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), more than 470 local anti-LGBTQ bills have been introduced in 2023 by members of the conservative [Republican Party](32070.md), including more than 190 that are specifically anti-trans. While in past years conservatives have focused on bathroom bills and banning trans athletes from sports, most bills passed in 2023 have focused on banning gender-affirming health care. Critics noted that these events are a part of the wider [culture war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_war#United_States) between liberals and conservatives in the U.S.[^cite_note-90]

## Hate crime laws

Main article: [Hate crime laws in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crime_laws_in_the_United_States)



Current U.S. LGBTQ [hate crimes laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crimes_laws_in_the_United_States) by state. A national hate crimes law encompasses both sexual orientation and gender identity.&#x20;

&#x20; Sexual orientation and gender identity recognized in state hate crimes law

&#x20; Sexual orientation recognized in state hate crimes law

&#x20; State hate crimes law uninclusive of sexual orientation or gender identity

Hate crime laws (also known as *bias crimes laws*) protect against crimes motivated by feelings of enmity against a protected class. Until 2009, a [federal law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crime_laws_in_the_United_States#Title_I_of_the_Civil_Rights_Act_of_1968) defined hate crimes committed on the basis of a person's race, color, religion, or nation origin when engaging in a federally protected activity. In October 2009, Congress passed the [Matthew Shepard Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Shepard_Act), which expanded the definition of hate crimes to include gender, sexual orientation, gender identity, and disability.[^cite_note-91] It removed the requirement that the victim of a hate crime be engaged in a federally protected activity.[^cite_note-92] Obama signed the legislation on October 28, 2009.[^cite_note-93]

Two statutes, the [Hate Crime Statistics Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_Crime_Statistics_Act) (1990) and the [Campus Hate Crimes Right to Know Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crime_laws_in_the_United_States#Campus_Hate_Crimes_Right_to_Know_Act_of_1997) (1997), require the [Department of Justice](52563.md) and the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI), as well as college/university campus security authorities, to collect and publish hate crime statistics.

As of December 2023, 46 states, the District of Columbia, Puerto Rico, the US Virgin Islands, and the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands have statutes criminalizing various types of bias-motivated violence or intimidation (the exceptions are [Arkansas](1930.md), [Georgia](48830.md), [South Carolina](27956.md), and [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming)). Each of these statutes covers bias on the basis of race, religion, and ethnicity; 34 cover disability; 34 of them cover sexual orientation; 28 cover gender; 13 cover age; 23 cover gender identity; five cover political affiliation.[^cite_note-adl-94] 31 states and the District of Columbia have statutes creating a [civil cause of action](1210255.md), in addition to the criminal penalty, for similar acts.[^cite_note-adl-94] Twenty-seven states and the District of Columbia have statutes requiring the state to collect hate crime statistics; 16 of these cover sexual orientation.[^cite_note-adl-94]

In *[Wisconsin v. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisconsin_v._Mitchell)* (1993) the Supreme Court unanimously held that state penalty-enhancement laws for hate crimes were constitutional and did not violate [First Amendment](31653.md) rights to freedom of thought and expression.

Laws that prohibit [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech_laws_by_country), including those that relate to sexual orientation or gender identity, are considered [unconstitutional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionality), due to the [First Amendment's](31653.md) broad protections for [free speech](21401843.md).[^cite_note-95]

### Gay panic defense

Main article: [Gay panic defense §  United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_panic_defense#_United_States)

#### Federal laws

In 2018, Senator [Edward Markey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Markey) (D-MA) and Representative [Joseph Kennedy III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Kennedy_III) (D-MA) introduced S.3188 and H.R.6358, respectively, which would prohibit a federal criminal defendant from asserting, as a defense, that the nonviolent sexual advance or an individual or a perception or belief of the gender, gender identity, or expression, or sexual orientation of an individual excuses or justifies conduct or mitigates the severity of an offense. Both bills died in committee. In June 2019, the bill was reintroduced in both houses of Congress as the Gay and Trans Panic Defense Prohibition Act of 2019 (S.1721 and H.R.3133). It has been reintroduced in both the 2021 and 2023 sessions.

#### State laws

As of October 2023, 17 states and the District of Columbia have banned the so-called "gay panic defense": California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, Hawaii, Illinois, Maine, Maryland, Nevada, New Hampshire New Jersey, New York, Oregon, Rhode Island, Vermont, Virginia, Washington.[^cite_note-96]

## Military service



Transgender military service by country and territory&#x20;

&#x20; Allows transgender military service

&#x20; Prohibits transgender military service

&#x20; Unknown if transgender military service allowed

&#x20; Has no military

Main article: [Sexual orientation and gender identity in the United States military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_and_gender_identity_in_the_United_States_military)

See also: [Don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell); [Don't Ask, Don't Tell Repeal Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell_Repeal_Act_of_2010); [Transgender personnel in the United States military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_personnel_in_the_United_States_military); [Sexual orientation in the United States military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_in_the_United_States_military); [Sexual orientation and gender identity in the United States military § Cross-dressers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_and_gender_identity_in_the_United_States_military#Cross-dressers); and [Intersex people in the United States military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex_people_in_the_United_States_military)

Although the U.S. military discharged soldiers for homosexual acts throughout the eighteenth and nineteenth century, U.S. military law did not expressly prohibit homosexuality or homosexual conduct until February 4, 1921. On May 5, 1950, the [Uniform Code of Military Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniform_Code_of_Military_Justice) was passed by Congress and signed into law by President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman), and became effective on May 31, 1951. Article 125 forbade sodomy among all military personnel, defining it as "any person subject to this chapter who engages in unnatural carnal copulation with another person of the same or opposite sex or with an animal is guilty of sodomy. Penetration, however slight, is sufficient to complete the offence." Application of Article 125 was severely limited by the 2003 U.S. Supreme Court decision in *Lawrence v. Texas,* and it was amended only to apply to 'forcible sodomy' on December 26, 2013, when President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) signed into law the [National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2014). The [National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2017) finally repealed the article and its classification of sodomy, as a crime separate from rape, altogether.

Prior to 1993, lesbian and gay people were not permitted to serve in the U.S. military. Under the "[Don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell)" (DADT) policy enacted that year, they were permitted to do so only if they did not disclose their sexual orientation. The [Don't Ask, Don't Tell Repeal Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell_Repeal_Act_of_2010) permitted homosexual men and women to serve openly in the armed forces once designated government officials certified that the military was prepared for the repeal.[^cite_note-97] Since September 20, 2011, gays, lesbians, and bisexuals have been able to serve openly.[^cite_note-98]

On July 13, 2015, Defense Secretary [Ashton Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton_Carter) said that the current regulations banning transgender individuals from serving were outdated, and announced a six-month study to determine if lifting the ban would have any impact on the military's effectiveness.[^cite_note-99] On June 30, 2016, Carter announced that the ban on transgender troops from openly serving had been lifted.[^cite_note-transgenderend-100] The policy went into effect on October 1, 2016, and training on transgender issues was scheduled to begin a month later.

On October 24, 2016, 10 soldiers in the [United States Army](32087.md) became the first to openly petition for a sex change since the ban on service by transgender individuals was lifted.[^cite_note-aptrans-101] The military was originally scheduled to complete its adjustment to openly transgender troops by July 2017.[^cite_note-aptrans-101] That month, however, Trump declared in a tweet that transgender people would be prohibited from serving in the military.[^cite_note-102] The next day, [Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff](364458.md) General [Joseph Dunford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Dunford) said, "There will be no modifications to the current policy until the President's direction has been received by the Secretary of Defense and the Secretary has issued implementation guidance. In the meantime, we will continue to treat all of our personnel with respect."[^cite_note-103]

Trump later published a memo on August 25, 2017, directing that an implementation plan be submitted to him by the Secretary of Defense and the Secretary of Homeland Security by February 2018.[^cite_note-Trump-170825-104] In November 2018, the Trump administration formally asked the Supreme Court to issue a ruling on the matter, even though lower courts were still hearing appeals.[^cite_note-105] Though the Supreme Court initially refused this request, on January 22, 2019, it granted temporary permission to the Trump administration to proceed with its ban,[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] and on March 12 the Department of Defense released a memorandum describing the terms of the ban to take effect on April 12, 2019.[^cite_note-108]

The memorandum offers some protection for existing military personnel who were already diagnosed with "[gender dysphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_dysphoria)" or who were already serving in their self-designated gender before the memorandum was issued.[^cite_note-109] However, new personnel must serve in their birth gender/sex and are disqualified from service if they have a recent history of gender dysphoria or if they have ever received hormones and surgery related to [gender transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_transition). Two bipartisan bills in Congress are pushing back against the ban.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]



Retired U.S. Air Force Maj. Laura Perry in 2016. Picture removed online by the Trump administration in 2025.

As of January 20, 2020, [transgender women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_woman) and other individuals [assigned male at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_assignment) were still required to sign-up for the [Selective Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selective_Service_System).[^cite_note-112]

On January 25, 2021, the new [Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration) revoked Trump's ban through an executive order, reverting the policy regarding [transgender people and military service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_people_and_military_service) to what it was in 2016 under the [Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obama_administration). Transgender personnel are now allowed to serve in the military under varying conditions and requirements. On April 30, 2021, the United States Department of Defense enacted a new policy calling for better medical access and gender marker assistance to transgender people serving in the United States Military.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114]

As of May 8, 2025, transgender individuals are banned from enlisting in and serving in the U.S. military, except under narrow waivers for those who have not undergone gender transition, have maintained stability in their birth sex for at least 36 consecutive months, serve in roles critical to warfighting capabilities, and are willing to adhere to all standards associated with their birth sex. Transgender civilian employees at the DoD and private military companies are not subject to the military ban.

## Prison

### Solitary confinement

More than 8,400 [detained migrants](27272402.md)—over a five-year period spanning both the Obama and Trump administrations—were placed in solitary confinement, which remains an ongoing practice as of May 2019. In half of the cases, detainees were being punished, but in the other half, the confinement was due to the person's mental illness, physical disability, or sexual orientation. Journalists identified six suicides among this population.[^cite_note-115]

### Conjugal visits

Main article: [Conjugal visits in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugal_visit#United_States)

In the United States, four states permit [conjugal visits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugal_visits) to prisoners: California, Connecticut, New York, and Washington;[^cite_note-116] all of these U.S. states have legalized [same-sex marriage](92656.md) in June 2015.[^cite_note-117] In June 2007, California, following the enactment in 2005 of a state law requiring state agencies to provide the same rights to domestic partners as to married couples, became the first U.S. state to allow same-sex conjugal visits. The new rules allowed for visits only by registered same-sex married couples or domestic partners, provided that the same-sex marriage or domestic partnership was established before the prisoner was incarcerated.[^cite_note-118] In New York, prior to the vote on same-sex visits, this state allowed 27 out of its 60 facilities to allow same-sex conjugal visits, but this law was not enforced state wide until April 2011. In 2014, both New Mexico and Mississippi banned conjugal visits.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-Sanburn-120]

## Transgender rights in the United States

Main article: [Transgender rights in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights_in_the_United_States)

From April 2022 to January 2025, [United States passports](3104779.md) gave the sex/gender options of male, female and X by self determination.[^cite_note-economist.com-121] Discrimination rates are very high for the transgender community and especially for transgender people of color.[^cite_note-122] Some frequent examples of discrimination and other forms of oppression faced by the transgender community are violence and hate crimes,[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] homelessness,[^cite_note-125] poverty,[^cite_note-126] sexual assault,[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] housing discrimination,[^cite_note-130] employment discrimination,[^cite_note-131] harassment,[^cite_note-132] bullying,[^cite_note-133] disproportionate rates of arrest and incarceration,[^cite_note-advocate.com-134] prison and immigration violence and mistreatment,[^cite_note-135] airport security humiliation,[^cite_note-nbcnews.com-136][^cite_note-msnbc.com-137] HIV/AIDS[^cite_note-138] and health disparities,[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] governmental/bureaucratic barriers to transitioning (documents and surgery requirements),[^cite_note-141] economic and societal barriers to transitioning (the high costs of medical care and the frequent denial of care),[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] to name only a few.

Some who experience exclusion from the workforce, turn to survival crimes, such as sex work, in order to have an income as a direct result of economic oppression and discrimination.[^cite_note-advocate.com-134] With the passage of the [Fight Online Sex Trafficking Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fight_Online_Sex_Trafficking_Act_\(FOSTA\)) (FOSTA) and [Stop Enabling Sex Traffickers Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stop_Enabling_Sex_Traffickers_Act) (SESTA) bills, those individuals who turn to sex work are put in more danger because they are forced to turn back to more dangerous methods of finding work, such as through pimps and working on the streets, than online forums where they were able to vet clients.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148]

Frequently, the media, and politicians sensationalize transgender identities and oppression is reinforced. Aware of this trend, in 2016, a coalition of over 250 anti-sexual assault and domestic violence organizations have released a joint letter decrying the trend of portraying transgender people in restrooms as sexual predators as untrue and harmful. Likewise, GLAAD has released a media guide[^cite_note-149] for reporters covering restroom usage in relation to the transgender community.

In 2022, over 230 anti-transgender bills were introduced in state legislatures in a coordinated national campaign to target transgender rights,[^cite_note-150] and over 350 in 2023.[^cite_note-151] Many of these bills became law.

Many transgender advocates also advocate for converting single-occupant, gender-segregated restrooms into single-occupant, all-gender restrooms by simply changing the signs due to the high rates of harassment and even violence faced by the transgender community when accessing gender-segregated restrooms according to their gender expression. All-gender/gender-neutral restrooms are also beneficial to nursing mothers, parents with different-sex children, and people with disabilities.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] Transgender advocates affirm all-gender restrooms as well as access to gendered restrooms as a matter of choice, safety, privacy, and dignity.[^cite_note-154]

### Identity documents



Legal requirements each state has for altering the sex on one's birth certificate as of June 2025.&#x20;

&#x20; State updates birth certificates using an administrative process and does not require provider documentation (15 states)

&#x20; State updates birth certificates using an administrative process or a court order and requires provider documentation of "appropriate treatment" (18 states, 3 territories + D.C.)

&#x20; State updates birth certificates, but it requires either an administrative process or a court order with proof of surgery (10 states, 2 territories)

&#x20; State does not allow for amending the gender marker on the birth certificate (7 states)

[An executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defending_Women_from_Gender_Ideology_Extremism_and_Restoring_Biological_Truth_to_the_Federal_Government) signed in the first hours of President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s second administration defined, for all purposes of the [federal government](195149.md), sex as binary, removing the federal recognition of non binary genders. It is also expected to prevent the federal government from allowing gender changes by people who are transgender.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157]

Different procedures and requirements for legal name changes and gender marker changes on [birth certificates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_certificate), [drivers licenses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drivers_license), social security identification and [passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passport) exist and can be inconsistent. Many states require [gender reassignment surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_reassignment_surgery) to change their name and gender marker. Also, documents that do not match each other can present difficulties in conducting personal affairs - particularly those which require multiple, matching forms of identification. Furthermore, having documents that do not match a person's gender presentation has been reported to lead to [harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harassment) and [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination).

#### Birth certificates

U.S. states make their own laws about [birth certificates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_certificate), and state courts have issued varied rulings about transgender people.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159]

Most states permit the name and sex to be changed on a birth certificate, either by amending the existing birth certificate or by issuing a new one, although some require medical proof of sex reassignment surgery to do so.

[Tennessee](30395.md) will not change the sex on a birth certificate at all, under any circumstances.[^cite_note-nbcnews.com2-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] In February 2020, the Idaho House of Representatives passed a similar bill.[^cite_note-163] [Oklahoma](22489.md) banned gender change in 2021.[^cite_note-164] In 2022, [Montana](19978.md) banned gender change.[^cite_note-165]

#### Drivers' licenses

As of June 2025, all U.S. States except for Kansas, Texas, Tennessee, and Florida allow the gender marker to be changed on a driver's license, although the requirements for doing so vary by state. Often, the requirements for changing one's driver's license are less stringent than those for changing the marker on the birth certificate. For example, until August 1, 2015, the state of [Massachusetts](1645518.md) required [sex reassignment surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_reassignment_surgery) for a birth certificate change,[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] but only a form including a sworn statement from a physician that the applicant is in fact the new gender to correct the sex designation on a driver's license.[^cite_note-168] As of November 2019, the Commonwealth of Massachusetts no longer requires any documentation or a sworn statement from a medical doctor in order to change one's gender marker on their drivers license/state ID. In order to change the gender marker, one only needs to fill out a new drivers license/ID card application reflecting the correct information.[^cite_note-169] The state of [Virginia](32432.md) had policies similar to those of Massachusetts, requiring [Sex reassignment surgery (SRS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_reassignment_surgery) for a birth certificate change, but not for a driver's license change.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] Virginia removed the requirement for surgery to change the gender marker in September 2020.[^cite_note-virginia.gov-172]

Sometimes, the states' requirements and laws conflict with and are dependent on each other; for example, a transgender woman who was born in Tennessee but living in Kentucky will be unable to have the gender marker changed on her Kentucky driver's license. This is due to the fact that Kentucky requires an amended birth certificate reflecting the person's accurate gender, but the state of Tennessee does not change gender markers on birth certificates at all.[^cite_note-173]

On July 1, 2023, Kansas Senate Bill 180 went into effect, mandating that gender markers on birth certificates and driver's licenses reflect a person's sex at birth – reversing a 2019 federal equal protection lawsuit settlement which allowed birth certificates to be changed to reflect a person's gender identity.[^cite_note-174]

In January 2024, Florida banned changing the gender marker on driver's licenses. Additionally, any person "misrepresenting" their gender would be subject to criminal and civil penalties.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

In addition, a number of states and city jurisdictions have passed legislation to allow a third gender marker on official identification documents (see below).

#### Cases

In May 2015, six Michigan transgender people filed *[Love v. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_v._Johnson)* in the [United States District Court for the Eastern District of Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Eastern_District_of_Michigan), challenging the state's policy requiring the information on a person's driver's license match the information on their birth certificate.[^cite_note-AlHajal-177][^cite_note-ACLUJohnsonFiled-178] This policy requires transgender people to change the information on their birth certificates in order to change their driver's licenses, which at the time of filing was not possible in Tennessee, Nebraska and Ohio, where three of the plaintiffs were born, and requires a court order in South Carolina, where a fourth was born. The remaining two residents were born in Michigan, and would be required to undergo surgery to change their birth certificates.[^cite_note-AlHajal-177] The plaintiffs in the case are represented by the [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union).[^cite_note-AlHajal-177][^cite_note-ACLUJohnsonFiled-178]

In November 2015, Judge [Nancy Edmunds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Edmunds) denied the State of Michigan's [motion to dismiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_to_dismiss) the case.[^cite_note-AlHajal-177]

#### Passports

The U.S. [State Department](31975.md) determines what identifying biographical information is placed on passports. On June 10, 2010, the policy on gender changes was amended to allow permanent gender marker changes to be made with the statement of a physician that "the applicant has had appropriate clinical treatment for gender transition to the new gender.";.[^cite_note-179] The previous policy required a statement from a surgeon that gender reassignment surgery was completed.[^cite_note-180] From April 11, 2022 to January 21, 2025, American passports gave the sex/gender options of male, female and X by self determination.[^cite_note-economist.com-121][^cite_note-181]

Since January 2025, trans and non-binary people have been prevented from altering their gender markers on passports, despite a court ruling to the contrary.

#### Third gender option

&#x20; X designation unavailable

Main article: [Legal recognition of non-binary gender § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_recognition_of_non-binary_gender#United_States)

From 2022 to 2025, the U.S. federal government recognizes a third gender option on passports. Other countries including [Australia](4689264.md), [New Zealand](4913064.md), [India](14533.md), [Nepal](171166.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Bangladesh](3454.md), [Germany](11867.md), [Malta](19137.md), and [Canada](5042916.md) have begun recognizing this.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-guardian-31aug2017-186] Third genders have traditionally been acknowledged in a number of [Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) cultures as "[two spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_spirit)" people, in traditional Hawaiian culture as the [māhū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81h%C5%AB), and as the [fa'afafine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fa'afafine) in [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa).[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] Similarly, immigrants from traditional cultures that acknowledge a [third gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_gender) would benefit from such a reform, including the [muxe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muxe) gender in southern Mexico and the [hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijra_\(South_Asia\)) of south Asian cultures.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

On June 10, 2016, an Oregon circuit court ruled that a resident, [Elisa Rae Shupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisa_Rae_Shupe), could obtain a non-binary gender designation. The [Transgender Law Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_Law_Center) believes this to be "the first ruling of its kind in the U.S."[^cite_note-oregon-194]

### Gender-affirming care bans

Main article: [Legal status of gender-affirming healthcare § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_status_of_gender-affirming_healthcare#United_States)

Further information: [Transgender rights in the United States § Healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights_in_the_United_States#Healthcare)

Gender-affirming care for minors has been available in the U.S. for more than a decade and is endorsed by major medical associations, but it has increasingly come under attack in many conservative legislatures.[^cite_note-195] Of the approximately 1.6 million Americans who are transgender, about 300,000 are under the age of 18.[^cite_note-196] According to the ACLU, in 2023 alone, over 500 anti-LGBTQ bills were submitted in the US, over 130 of which were about healthcare.[^cite_note-mapping_attacks-197] Efforts to prohibit gender-affirming care for minors had begun several years earlier, but did not receive much attention from state legislatures until more recently.[^cite_note-ual2-198] The conservative organization [Do No Harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Do_No_Harm_\(organization\)) was influential in developing model legislation that appeared starting in 2022 in Arkansas, Florida, Iowa, Mississippi, Montana, New Hampshire, and West Virginia legislatures.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]

Many Democrat-controlled states have gone in the opposite direction and enacted laws protecting access to gender affirming care for minors and adults. These laws, often called "shield" laws, often explicitly combine protections for gender-affirming care and abortion and cover a variety of protections, including protecting both providers and patients from being punished, mandating insurance providers to cover the procedures, and acting as "sanctuary states" that protect patients traveling to the state from other states that have banned such treatments among other provisions.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202]

Bans on gender-affirming care have been criticized as governmental interference with the [doctor–patient relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor%E2%80%93patient_relationship), and taking away healthcare decisions from parents and families for their children.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] State level bans on gender-affirming care in the United States have led some families with transgender children to move out of their states.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] As of October 2023, approximately 105,200 transgender youth aged 13 to 17 lived in states where gender affirming care is banned for minors. However, around 26,000 of those youth are currently still able to access care in their state due to court orders that prohibit enforcement of the laws. Conversely, around 146,700 transgender youth live in states with shield laws.[^cite_note-williamsinstitute-208] An analysis from [KFF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser_Family_Foundation) in late January 2024 estimated that 38% of trans youth between the ages of 13–17 in the United States lived in states with laws limiting youth access to gender-affirming care.[^cite_note-KFF-209]

The [American Psychological Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Psychological_Association) approved a policy statement in February 2024 supporting unobstructed access to health care and evidence-based clinical care for transgender, gender-diverse, and nonbinary children, adolescents, and adults, as well as opposing state bans and policies intended to limit access to such care.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211]

In May 2024, Tennessee became the first state to prohibit helping a child access gender-affirming care without the consent of their parent or guardian. The child's parents are allowed to sue the person who assisted the pursuit of gender-affirming care. There is no criminal penalty.[^cite_note-212]

On October 17, 2024, Texas attorney general [Ken Paxton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Paxton) filed suit against a doctor who allegedly provided gender-affirming care to 21 minors after the treatments had been banned for minors in Texas, the first time that such a suit has been brought in the U.S.[^cite_note-213]

On March 10, 2026, the [United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Fourth_Circuit) ruled that West Virginia may continue to prohibit gender-affirming procedures for adults from being covered by [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid), arguing that the law applied to procedures and not individuals, and that it was "not irrational for a legislature to encourage citizens to appreciate their sex".[^cite_note-214]

#### Bans for minors

As of August 2025, 27 states had enacted some form of ban on gender-affirming care for minors, 19 of which were enacted in 2023.[^cite_note-williamsinstitute-208][^cite_note-215] However, 16 of these bans are being challenged in court as of January 2024.[^cite_note-KFF-209] Furthermore, only 21 of the 27 states have complete bans which are fully in effect. Four states have only partial bans and two are currently blocked from taking effect. While some states have banned all forms of medical transition, others such as Arizona, Nebraska and Georgia have banned only specific types such as hormone therapy or surgery. Seven states have exceptions which allow minors who were already receiving gender affirming care prior to the ban to continue their treatments.[^cite_note-KFF-209]

&#x20;Currently, all 27 states make exceptions for puberty blockers, hormones and surgery for cisgender and intersex children.[^cite_note-KFF-209] There is only one state, Missouri, that has a ban which is set to expire after a certain period of time. Nearly all states with restrictions include specific provisions with penalties for providers and 4 states include provisions directed at parents or guardians.[^cite_note-KFF-209] An additional 4 states include laws/policies that impact school officials such as teachers and counselors, among others.[^cite_note-KFF-209]
