| [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Louisiana) | State Legislature |  | January 1, 2024 | On June 29, 2023, [John Bel Edwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bel_Edwards) vetoed a ban on blockers, hormones, and surgery for minors. On July 18, the [Louisiana State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_State_Legislature) overrode his veto. |
| [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Oklahoma) | Governor Kevin Stitt | May 1, 2023 |  | On May 1, 2023, Governor [Kevin Stitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Stitt) signed a bill that makes it a felony for doctors to provide gender-transition medical care for anyone under the age of 18.[^cite_note-277] In October 2023, a judge declined to stop the law from taking effect.[^cite_note-278] |
| [Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Nebraska) | Governor Jim Pillen | October 2, 2023 | October 2, 2023 | On October 2, 2023, the state Department of Health and Human Services announced that Republican Governor [Jim Pillen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Pillen) had approved emergency regulations banning gender affirming surgeries for minors. Puberty blockers and hormone treatments for minors still remain legal, however applicants must now wait seven days and undergo at least 40 hours of "clinically neutral" therapy before starting them. The new regulations went into effect immediately.[^cite_note-279] |
| [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Ohio) | State legislature |  | April 30, 2025 | On January 5, 2024, Governor [Mike DeWine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_DeWine) signed an executive order banning gender-affirming surgeries for minors.[^cite_note-280] Previously, on December 29, 2023, he had vetoed the Saving Adolescents from Experimentation (SAFE) Act (HB68) passed (mostly along party lines) by the [Ohio Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_Legislature) on December 13 which banned gender-affirming surgeries as well as hormones and puberty blockers for minors. The bill includes exceptions for this kind of care for non-transgender youth, and it allows children who were already receiving gender-affirming care in Ohio to continue their treatment.[^cite_note-281] On January 24, 2024, the legislature overrode DeWine's veto thereby making HB68 law.[^cite_note-282] On April 16, 2024, a judge temporarily blocked the ban from taking effect.[^cite_note-283] On August 6, 2024, a judge overturned the injunction and allowed the law to take effect immediately.[^cite_note-284] On March 18, 2025, the state's 10th District Court of Appeale reversed the judge's decision and reinstated the injunction.[^cite_note-285] On April 30, 2025, the [Supreme Court of Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Ohio) ruled 4-3 that the ban could take effect while further court battles play out.[^cite_note-286] |
| [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Wyoming) | Governor Mark Gordon | March 22, 2024 | July 1, 2024 | On March 22, 2024, Wyoming Governor [Mark Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Gordon) signed a law criminalizing trans health care for minors.[^cite_note-287] |
| [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_South_Carolina) | Governor Henry McMaster | May 21, 2024 | May 21, 2024 | On May 21, 2024, South Carolina Governor [Henry McMaster](3482397.md) signed a law banning trans health care for minors. The law, which went into effect immediately, also requires principals, teachers and other school staff members to tell parents when their children want to use a name other than their legal one, or pronouns that do not match their sex assigned at birth. It also bars adults under 26 from using Medicaid to cover the costs for trans health care.[^cite_note-288] This part of the bill is in direct opposition to a [4th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Fourth_Circuit) ruling from the month prior which ruled that state Medicaid bans on gender-affirming care in the 4th Circuit, which includes South Carolina, are unconstitutional.[^cite_note-289] |

The bill also protects providers from being sued for providing care.[^cite_note-314] |

The bill also contains protections against "abusive litigation", bans medical malpractice insurers and health carriers from taking adverse actions against gender-affirming care providers, and clarifies permitted disclosures of protected healthcare information in response to new federal regulations.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] |

### Bathroom bills

Main article: [Bathroom bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathroom_bill)

A bathroom bill is the common name for legislation or a statute that defines access to [public toilets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_toilet) by [gender](38076.md) (restrooms)—or transgender [individual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual). Bathroom bills affect access to sex-segregated public facilities for an individual based on a determination of their sex as defined in some specific way—such as their sex as [assigned at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_assignment), their sex as listed on their [birth certificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_certificate), or the sex that corresponds to their [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity).[^cite_note-vox-319] A bathroom bill can either be inclusive or exclusive of transgender individuals, depending on the aforementioned definition of their sex. [Unisex public toilets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unisex_public_toilets) are one option to overcome this controversy.

Critics of bills which exclude transgender individuals from restrooms which conform to their gender identity argue that they do not make public restrooms any safer for [cisgender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisgender) (non-transgender) people, and that they make public restrooms less safe for both transgender people and [gender non-conforming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_non-conforming) cisgender people.[^cite_note-mic.com-320][^cite_note-rolereboot-321][^cite_note-patheos-322] Additionally, critics claim there have been no cases of a transgender person attacking a cisgender person in a public restroom,[^cite_note-mic.com-320][^cite_note-323] although there has been at least one isolated incident of voyeurism in a fitting room.[^cite_note-NYT-324] By comparison, a much larger percentage of transgender people have been verbally, physically, and sexually harassed or attacked by cisgender people in public facilities.[^cite_note-usts-325] For these reasons the controversy over transgender bathroom access has been labeled a [moral panic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_panic).[^cite_note-psmag-326]

Proponents say such legislation is necessary to maintain privacy, protect what they claim to be an innate sense of modesty held by most cisgender people, prevent voyeurism, assault, molestation, and rape,[^cite_note-Guardian-2015-327] and retain psychological comfort.[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

One bathroom bill, the [Public Facilities Privacy & Security Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Facilities_Privacy_&_Security_Act) in [North Carolina](21650.md), was approved as a law in 2016, although portions of the measure were later repealed in 2017 as part of a compromise between the Democratic governor and Republican-controlled Legislature.

Public opinion regarding "transgender bathroom rights" in the United States is mixed, see summary table below.

### Trans athletes in sports



Map of current or proposed state laws which ban transgender athletes from participating in the sport of their gender identity:&#x20;

&#x20; Law enacted which bans trans athletes from participating in sport under their gender identity; enforces gender classifications in sports based on "original" registered biological sex

&#x20; Law preventing trans athletes from participating in sport in their gender identity enacted, but currently blocked from enforcement via court order

[^cite_note-330]

[^cite_note-hecox-331]

Twenty-seven states have banned transgender people from sports under their gender identity in various capacities. Alabama,[^cite_note-332] Arizona,[^cite_note-333] Arkansas,[^cite_note-334] Florida,[^cite_note-335] Georgia,[^cite_note-336] Idaho,**[^cite_note-337]** Indiana,[^cite_note-338] Iowa,[^cite_note-339] Kansas,[^cite_note-340] Kentucky,[^cite_note-341] Louisiana,[^cite_note-342] Mississippi,[^cite_note-343] Missouri[^cite_note-344] Montana,[^cite_note-345] Nebraska[^cite_note-346] New Hampshire,[^cite_note-auto2-347] North Carolina,[^cite_note-348] North Dakota,[^cite_note-349] Ohio,[^cite_note-350] Oklahoma,[^cite_note-351] South Carolina,[^cite_note-352] South Dakota,[^cite_note-353] Tennessee,[^cite_note-354] Texas,[^cite_note-355] Utah,[^cite_note-356] West Virginia,[^cite_note-357] and Wyoming.[^cite_note-358] The passage of legislation against transgender youth has seen increases in calls to [Trans Lifeline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_Lifeline), a suicide crisis hotline run by and for transgender people.[^cite_note-359] Some of these bans only apply to school sports and some only apply to transgender women, but not transgender men.

The Human Rights Campaign has argued that these discriminatory laws are not about protecting women's sports, but rather attempt to "undermine the existence of transgender people."[^cite_note-360] Transgender advocates have noted that hormone replacement therapy and testosterone suppression reduce muscle mass and physical strength in transgender women, reducing the possibility of a competitive advantage.[^cite_note-361] A 2021 literature review concluded that for trans women, even with testosterone suppression, "the data show that strength, lean body mass, muscle size and bone density are only trivially affected. The reductions observed in muscle mass, size, and strength are very small compared to the baseline differences between males and females in these variables, and thus, there are major performance and safety implications in sports where these attributes are competitively significant."[^cite_note-sportsmedicine-362] After 24 months of testosterone suppression, bone mass is generally preserved. The review states that no study has reported muscle loss greater than 12% with testosterone suppression even after three years of hormone therapy.[^cite_note-sportsmedicine-362] It found that trans women are in the top 10% of females regarding lean body mass and possess a grip 25% stronger than most females.[^cite_note-sportsmedicine-362] They suggest that instead of universal guidelines, each individual sport federation decide how to "balance between inclusion, safety and fairness" due to differences between sports.[^cite_note-sportsmedicine-362] Transgender inclusion in sports is supported by the [Women's Sports Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Sports_Foundation), the [Women's National Basketball Players Association](68077461.md) (WNBPA), the [National Women's Law Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Law_Center), and [Athlete Ally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athlete_Ally), as well as [United States Women's National Soccer Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Women's_National_Soccer_Team) Captain [Megan Rapinoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megan_Rapinoe), tennis legend [Billie Jean King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Jean_King), WNBA [Minnesota Lynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Lynx) coach [Cheryl Reeve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheryl_Reeve), and [WNBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA) star [Candace Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker).[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366]

The [United States Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Education) during the [Biden Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_Administration) has said transgender students are protected under Title IX[^cite_note-367] and during the [second Trump administration](78298108.md) has said schools allowing transgender participation in girls' sports is a Title IX violation.[^cite_note-368]

### Transgender people in prison

In September 2011, a California state court denied the request of a California inmate, Lyralisa Stevens, for sex reassignment surgery at the state's expense.[^cite_note-369]

On January 17, 2014, in *[Kosilek v. Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Kosilek#Incarceration_and_medical_lawsuits)* a three-judge panel of the [First Circuit Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_First_Circuit) ordered the [Massachusetts Department of Corrections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_Department_of_Corrections) to provide Michelle Kosilek, a Massachusetts inmate, with sex reassignment surgery. It said denying the surgery violated Kosilek's Eighth Amendment rights, which included "receiving medically necessary treatment ... even if that treatment strikes some as odd or unorthodox".[^cite_note-370]



Housing of Transgender Inmates in prisons, juvenile detentions, and jails by state according to statute, court ruling, or policy:&#x20;

&#x20; Allows transgender inmates to be housed according to their gender identity

&#x20; Allows transgender inmates to be housed in separate facilities apart from other inmates reserved for transgender people only

On April 3, 2015, the U.S. Department of Justice intervened in a federal lawsuit filed in [Georgia](48830.md) to argue that denying hormone treatment for transgender inmates violates their rights. It contended that the state's policy that only allows for continuing treatments begun before incarceration was insufficient and that inmate treatment needs to be based on ongoing assessments.[^cite_note-371] The case was brought by Ashley Diamond, an inmate who had used hormone treatment for seventeen years before entering the Georgia prison system.[^cite_note-372]

On May 11, 2018, the US Bureau of Prisons announced that prison guidelines issued by the Obama administration in January 2017 to allow transgender prisoners to be transferred to prisons housing inmates of the gender which they identify with had been rescinded and that assigned sex at birth would once again determine where transgender prisoners are jailed.[^cite_note-373]

As of January 2021, one state, [California](5407.md), allows transgender people to be housed in prison according to their gender identity, and another, [New Mexico](21649.md), has separate facilities entirely, which are segregated off from other inmates, and are reserved for transgender people only.

### Transgender Day of Remembrance

[Transgender Day of Remembrance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_Day_of_Remembrance) was founded in 1999 by [Gwendolyn Ann Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwendolyn_Ann_Smith) and [JMEL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JMEL?action=edit\&redlink=1) a transgender woman,[^cite_note-374] to memorialize the murder of transgender woman Rita Hester in [Allston, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allston,_Massachusetts).[^cite_note-375] It has slowly evolved from the [web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web)-based project started by Smith into an international day of action every November 20. Transgender Day of Remembrance is now a day to honor all transgender lives lost to murder caused by transphobia. Several communities and organizations all over the world have made vigils accessible to all for the lost lives through murder.[^cite_note-376]

## Intersex rights in the United States

Main article: [Intersex rights in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex_rights_in_the_United_States)

Since April 11, 2022, [United States passports](3104779.md) have given the sex/gender options of male, female and X by self determination.[^cite_note-economist.com-121] [Intersex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex) people in the United States have some of the same rights as others but with significant gaps, particularly in protection from non-consensual cosmetic medical interventions, violence, and discrimination.[^cite_note-elders2017-377][^cite_note-interacthealth-378] Many non-consensual medical surgeries are being performed to align these individuals with a more typically male or female sex when they are babies or children. Some are also put on hormones to ensure that their bodies develop to the sex they were assigned. In August 2018, the [California state legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_state_legislature) passed a resolution that condemns these types of surgeries.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] Actions by intersex civil society organizations aim to eliminate harmful practices and promote social acceptance and equality.[^cite_note-interactcat-383][^cite_note-384] In recent years, intersex activists have also secured some legal recognition.[^cite_note-ohara2016-385]

## Medical discrimination

### HIV/AIDS



HIV criminalization laws by state:&#x20;

&#x20; State has HIV criminalization law and may also require sex offender registration for HIV and AIDS exposure

&#x20; State has HIV criminalization law

&#x20; State has no HIV criminalization law, but does have HIV-specific sentencing enhancements for sex-related convictions

Main article: [HIV/AIDS in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_the_United_States)

In 1981, the AIDS epidemic, caused by the Human Immunodeficiency Virus, was reported in the United States. HIV is transferred through infected blood and sexual contact, and in the early 1980s, risk factors were reported to include homosexuality and drug use.[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] One of the earliest medical clinics treating the disease was run in New York City by Dr. [Joseph Sonnabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Sonnabend). By 1985, thousands of people in the United States had died. Many LGBTQ advocacy organizations began to speak up and raise funds.

### Blood and tissue donation

Main article: [Blood donation restrictions on men who have sex with men § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_donation_restrictions_on_men_who_have_sex_with_men#United_States)

In the United States, the [Food and Drug Administration](11632.md) (FDA) issues non-binding guidance for deferral of blood donations, which are universally followed.[^cite_note-388] In May 2023, the restrictions were updated to focus on behavior rather than sexual orientation or gender. People are ineligible to donate blood if they have:[^cite_note-2023_recommendations-389]

- Had anal sex within the past 3 months and have a new or multiple sexual partners

- Ever tested positive for HIV or been treated for HIV with [anti-retroviral therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-retroviral_therapy)

- Taken any HIV prevention medication ([PEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-exposure_prophylaxis) or [PrEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-exposure_prophylaxis); these can affect test results) by mouth within the past 3 months or by injection within the past 2 years

- Ever exchanged sex for payment or barter

- Used non-prescription injection drugs within the past 3 months

- Had sex within the past 3 months with someone who has ever tested positive for HIV

- Received an allogeneic blood transfusion or been exposed to blood of another individual (e.g. through a wound) within the past 3 months

- Gotten a tattoo or body piercing within the last 3 months, unless pierced with single-use equipment or tattooed in a state-approved shop with sterile needles and non-reused ink

- Been infected with or treated for syphilis or gonorrhea within the past 3 months

- [Hemophilia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemophilia) or other clotting factor deficiency

Current regulations prohibit tissue and semen donation by any man who has had sex with another man in the preceding five years, even if all infectious testing is negative.[^cite_note-390] This five-year MSM deferral policy prevents thousands of donations of eye tissue annually, despite a global shortage of donated eye tissue needed for vision-restoring [corneal transplant surgeries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corneal_transplantation).[^cite_note-391]

### Conversion therapy

Main article: [List of U.S. jurisdictions banning conversion therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._jurisdictions_banning_conversion_therapy)

[Conversion therapy](26410.md) is the discredited practice of attempting to change a person's sexual orientation or gender identity.[^cite_note-392] 21 states, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico ban the practice of conversion therapy on minors.[^cite_note-393]

## History of U.S. Supreme Court decisions on LGBTQ rights

See also: [List of LGBT-related cases in the United States Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_LGBT-related_cases_in_the_United_States_Supreme_Court)

In March 1956, a [Federal District Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_District_Court) ruled that *ONE: The Homosexual Magazine*, was obscene under the Federal [Comstock laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comstock_laws) and thus could not be sent through the United States Postal Service. This ruling was upheld by the [9th Circuit Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Circuit_Court_of_Appeals), but in 1958, the Supreme Court issued a landmark ruling in *[One, Inc. v. Olesen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One,_Inc._v._Olesen)*, [355](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_cases,_volume_355) [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports) [371](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/355/371/) (1958), which overturned the previous rulings under a new legal precedent that had been established by the landmark case, *[Roth v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roth_v._United_States)*, [354](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_cases,_volume_354) [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports) [476](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/354/476/) (1957). As a result, gay newspapers, magazines and other publications could be lawfully distributed through the public mail service. On May 22, 1967, the Supreme Court upheld the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1952) in *[Boutilier v. Immigration and Naturalization Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boutilier_v._Immigration_and_Naturalization_Service)* [387](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_cases,_volume_387) [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports) [118](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/387/118/) (1967). The Court held that barring homosexuals from entry to the United States was valid and constitutional. This ban remained in effect until 1991 after the passage of the [Immigration Act of 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1990).[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396]

In 1972, a [Tacoma, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma,_Washington) teacher of twelve years with a perfect record was terminated after a former student [outed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outing) him to the vice-principal. The [Washington Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Supreme_Court) found that homosexuality was immoral and impaired his efficiency as a teacher. The court supported its conclusion in various ways, including the definition of homosexuality in the New Catholic Encyclopedia, the criminal nature of homosexual conduct, and finding that an "immoral" person could not be trusted to instruct students as his presence would be inherently disruptive. On October 3, 1977, the Supreme Court denied [certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certiorari), although Justices Brennan and Marshall would have granted cert. This was the first homosexual discrimination decision to be aired on national network news. In fact, it was simultaneously aired on all three national network evening news shows, reaching approximately 60 million viewers.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401]

In 1985, the Supreme Court heard *[Board of Education v. National Gay Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gay_Task_Force_v._Board_of_Education)*, which concerned First and Fourteenth Amendment challenges against a law that allowed schools to fire teachers for public homosexual conduct.[^cite_note-402] The Court affirmed the lower court by an equally divided vote 4–4 allowing the Tenth Circuit's ruling that partially struck down the law to stand without setting precedent.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404]

Also in 1985, the Supreme Court refused to hear an appeal of *[Gay Student Services v. Texas A\&M University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_Student_Services_v._Texas_A\&M_University)*, letting stand an appellate ruling ordering the university to provide official recognition of a student organization for homosexual students.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406]

On June 30, 1986, the Supreme Court of the United States ruled in *[Bowers v. Hardwick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowers_v._Hardwick)*, that same-sex intimate conduct was not protected under the [right to privacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_privacy) established under the [Fourteenth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourteenth_Amendment_of_the_United_States_Constitution).

On May 20, 1996, the Supreme Court of the United States ruled in *[Romer v. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romer_v._Evans)* against an amendment to the Colorado state constitution that would have prevented any city, town or county in the state from taking any legislative, executive, or judicial action to protect homosexual or bisexual citizens from discrimination on the basis of their sexual orientation.

On March 4, 1998, the Supreme Court of the United States ruled in *[Oncale v. Sundowner Offshore Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oncale_v._Sundowner_Offshore_Services)* that federal laws banning on-the-job [sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment) also applied when both parties are the same sex. The lower courts, however, have reached differing conclusions about whether this ruling applies to harassment motivated by anti-gay animus.

On June 28, 2000, the Supreme Court of the United States ruled in *[Boy Scouts of America v. Dale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_Scouts_of_America_v._Dale)* that the [Boy Scouts of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_Scouts_of_America) had a First Amendment right to exclude people from its organization on the basis of [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation), irrespective of any applicable civil rights laws.

On June 26, 2003, the Supreme Court ruled in *[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)* that intimate consensual sexual conduct is part of the liberty protected by substantive [due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process) under the [Fourteenth Amendment](31666.md). The majority opinion, written by Justice [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), explicitly overruled *Bowers v. Hardwick,* a 1986 decision that found sodomy laws to be constitutional.[^cite_note-OC-35]

Ten years after the *Lawrence* decision, the Supreme Court ruled on June 26, 2013, by a 5–4 vote in *[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)* that section 3 of the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), that forbade the federal government from recognizing lawfully performed same-sex marriages, was found to violate the [Fifth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution). The federal government then began to recognize lawfully performed same-sex marriages, and provide federal rights, privileges and benefits.[^cite_note-CftxP-407][^cite_note-Opinion-408]



A man waves the [rainbow flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_flag_\(LGBT\)) outside the Supreme Court during *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*

On June 26, 2015, the Supreme Court ruled in *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)* that same-sex marriage cannot be prohibited by a state. Consequently, same-sex marriages are licensed and recognized as valid and enforced in all states and areas subject to the jurisdiction of the U.S. Constitution.

On June 15, 2020, the Supreme Court of the United States ruled that the protections provided by Title VII of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) are also extended to LGBTQ individuals, thereby making it illegal for workplaces with 15 or more employees to discriminate on the basis of sexuality or gender identity.[^cite_note-409] The three consolidated cases were *[Altitude Express, Inc. v. Zarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altitude_Express,_Inc._v._Zarda)*; *[Bostock v. Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County)*; and *[R.G. & G.R. Harris Funeral Homes Inc. v. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R.G._&_G.R._Harris_Funeral_Homes_Inc._v._Equal_Employment_Opportunity_Commission)*. *USA Today* afterwards stated that in addition to LGBTQ employment discrimination, "The court's ruling is likely to have a sweeping impact on federal civil rights laws barring sex discrimination in education, health care, housing and financial credit."[^cite_note-410]

On June 30, 2023, the Supreme Court ruled by a 6–3 vote in *[303 Creative LLC v. Elenis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/303_Creative_LLC_v._Elenis)* that businesses can refuse to create works that recognize [same-sex marriage](92656.md) if it goes against their values, on the basis of the [First Amendment](31653.md)'s protection of free speech.[^cite_note-411]

On March 31, 2026, the Supreme Court ruled in *[Chiles v. Salazar](79425861.md),* by an 8–1 vote, that the First Amendment prohibits states from using their licensing authority to restrict the topics therapists may discuss with clients, striking down Colorado's ban on conversion therapy for minors.[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-413]

On June 30, 2026, the Supreme Court ruled in the consolidated cases *[Little v. Hecox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_v._Hecox)* and *[West Virginia v. B. P. J.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Virginia_v._B._P._J.)*, holding that the Idaho and West Virginia laws barring transgender girls and women from playing on female school athletic teams do not violate the [Equal Protection Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Protection_Clause) or [Title IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX).[^cite_note-414]

## History of LGBTQ rights under U.S. presidents

See also: [LGBTQ history in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_history_in_the_United_States)

### George Washington

#### Commander-in-Chief of the Continental Army

To train the new American Army in the latest military drills and tactics, General [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) brought in [Friedrich Wilhelm von Steuben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_von_Steuben) (1730–94), who had been an officer on the German General staff. Von Steuben escaped Germany where he was threatened with prosecution for homosexuality. He joined Washington's army at Valley Forge in February 1778 accompanied by two young aides. Steuben became an American general, and a senior advisor to Washington. Despite rumors about sexual behavior at his parties, there never was an investigation of Steuben, and he received a congressional pension after the war.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416]

The first evidence of discrimination to homosexuals serving in the United States military dates from March 11, 1778, when Lieutenant [Frederick Gotthold Enslin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Gotthold_Enslin) was brought to trial before a court-martial. According to General Washington's report: "...Lieutt. Enslin of [Colo](199023.md). [Malcolm's Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm's_Additional_Continental_Regiment) tried for attempting to commit *sodomy* ..." Washington's secretary described the results of the trial: "His [Excellency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excellency) the [Commander in Chief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander_in_Chief) approves the sentence and with Abhorrence & Detestation of such Infamous Crimes orders Lieut. Enslin to be [drummed out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drumming_out) of Camp tomorrow morning...."[^cite_note-wash_vol_11-417]

### John Adams

In 1801, Congress enacted the [District of Columbia Organic Act of 1801](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Columbia_Organic_Act_of_1801) that continued all criminal laws of Maryland and Virginia in the now formally structured District, with those of Maryland applying to that portion of the District ceded from Maryland, and those of Virginia applying to that portion ceded from Virginia. At the time, Maryland had a sodomy law applicable only to free males with a punishment of "labour for any time, in their discretion, not exceeding seven years for the same crime, on the public roads of the said county, or in making, repairing or cleaning the streets or bason \[sic] of Baltimore-town;" it imposed the death penalty for slaves committing sodomy. Similarly, Virginia had a penalty of 1–10 years for free persons committing sodomy, but imposed the death penalty for slaves committing sodomy. The law went into effect on February 27, 1801.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### Thomas Jefferson

#### Governor of Virginia

In 1779, [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson) wrote a law in Virginia which contained a maximum punishment of [castration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castration) for men who engaged in sodomy.[^cite_note-419] However, what was intended by Jefferson as a liberalization of the sodomy laws in Virginia at that time was rejected by the Virginia Legislature, which continued to prescribe death as the maximum penalty for the crime of sodomy in that state.[^cite_note-420]

### Andrew Jackson

In 1831, Congress established penalties in the District of Columbia for a number of crimes, but not for sodomy. It specified that "every other felony, misdemeanor, or offence not provided for by this act, may and shall be punished as heretofore\[.]" At the time, Maryland and Virginia had a penalty of 1–10 years for committing sodomy. It went into effect on March 2, 1831.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### William Henry Harrison

#### Governor of the Indiana Territory

In 1807, [William Henry Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison) signed into law a comprehensive criminal code that included the first sodomy law for the [Indiana Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Territory) that eliminated the gender-specifics, reduced the penalty for a maximum of 1 to 5 years in prison, a fine of \$100 to \$500, up to 500 lashes on the back, and a permanent loss of civil rights.[^cite_note-421]

### Benjamin Harrison

In 1892, Congress passed a law for the District of Columbia that states that "for the preservation of the public peace and the protection of property within the District of Columbia." Labeled in the law as vagrants were "all public prostitutes, and all such persons who lead a notoriously lewd or lascivious course of life\[.]" All offenders had to post bond of up to \$200 for good behavior for a period of six months. The law went into effect on July 29, 1892.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### William McKinley

In 1898, Congress amended the provision by removing the word "notoriously" when concerning a lewd or lascivious course of life, thereby allowing prosecution of persons without the condition of notoriety. The bond for good behavior was raised to \$500, and the law was made gender-neutral. The law went into effect on July 8, 1898.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

In 1901, Congress adopted a new code for the District of Columbia that expressly recognized common-law crimes, with a penalty for them of up to five years and/or a \$1,000 fine. The law went into effect on March 3, 1901.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### Woodrow Wilson

On December 14, 1916, President [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) vetoed the [Immigration Act of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1917), which would have excluded individuals from entering the United States who were found "mentally defective" or who had a "constitutional psychopathic inferiority." A similar Public Health Service definition of homosexuals was used simultaneously by the Immigration and Naturalization Service (INS) to reinforce the language of the Immigration Act of 1917 and effectively ban all homosexual immigrants who disclosed their sexual minority status. On February 5, 1917, the Congress overrode Wilson's veto, implementing the Immigration Act of 1917 into law.[^cite_note-Sexual_Orientation_and_Asylum_in_the_United_States-422]

On March 1, 1917, the [Articles of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_War) of 1916 are implemented. This included a revision of the Articles of War of 1806, the new regulations detail statutes governing U.S. military discipline and justice. Under the category Miscellaneous Crimes and Offences, Article 93 states that any person subject to military law who commits "assault with intent to commit sodomy" shall be punished as a court-martial may direct.[^cite_note-usni-423]

On June 4, 1920, Congress modified Article 93 of the Articles of War of 1916. It was changed to make the act of sodomy itself a crime, separate from the offense of assault with intent to commit sodomy.[^cite_note-usni-423] It went into effect on February 4, 1921.[^cite_note-424]

### Franklin Roosevelt

#### Assistant Secretary of the Navy

In 1919, Democratic Assistant Secretary of the Navy [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) requested an investigation into "vice and depravity" in the sea services after a [sting operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sting_operation) in which undercover operatives attempt to seduce sailors suspected of being homosexual had already begun at the Naval base in Newport, Rhode Island. At least 17 sailors were jailed and court-martialed before public outcry prompted a Republican-led Senate committee to condemn the methods of the operation. Roosevelt denied he had any knowledge that entrapment had been used or that he would have approved of it.[^cite_note-425]

#### Presidency

In 1935, Congress passed a law for the District of Columbia that made it a crime for "any person to invite, entice, persuade, or to address for the purpose of inviting, enticing, or persuading any person or persons...to accompany, to go with, to follow him or her to his or her residence, or to any other house or building, inclosure, or other place, for the purpose of prostitution, or any other immoral or lewd purpose." It imposed a fine of up to \$100, up to 90 days in jail, and courts were permitted to "impose conditions" on anyone convicted under this law, including "medical and mental examination, diagnosis and treatment by proper public health and welfare authorities, and such other terms and conditions as the court may deem best for the protection of the community and the punishment, control, and rehabilitation of the defendant." The law went into effect on August 14, 1935.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

In 1941, Congress enacted a new solicitation law for the District of Columbia that labeled a "vagrant" any person who "engages in or commits acts of fornication or perversion for hire." The law went into effect on December 17, 1941.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### Harry Truman

In 1948, Congress enacted the first sodomy law in the District of Columbia, which established a penalty of up to 10 years in prison or a fine of up to \$1,000 for sodomy. Also included with this sodomy law was a psychopathic offender law and a law "to provide for the treatment of sexual psychopaths in the District of Columbia, and for other purposes." The law went into effect on June 9, 1948.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

On May 5, 1950, the [Uniform Code of Military Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniform_Code_of_Military_Justice) was passed by Congress and was signed into law by President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman), and became effective on May 31, 1951. Article 125 forbids sodomy among all military personnel, defining it as "any person subject to this chapter who engages in unnatural carnal copulation with another person of the same or opposite sex or with an animal is guilty of sodomy. Penetration, however slight, is sufficient to complete the offence."[^cite_note-usni-423]

On June 25, 1952, the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1952) was vetoed by President Truman because he regarded the bill as "un-American" and discriminatory. The bill prohibits "aliens afflicted with a psychopathic personality, epilepsy, or a mental defect" from entry into the United States.[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] Congress would later override his veto and implemented the act into law.[^cite_note-Sexual_Orientation_and_Asylum_in_the_United_States-422]

### Dwight D. Eisenhower

On April 27, 1953, President [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) signed [Executive Order 10450](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_10450) which prohibits Federal employees from being members of a group or organization considered subversive. The order lists "sexual perversion" as a security risk constituting grounds for termination or denial of employment. The order went into effect on May 27, 1953.[^cite_note-usni-423]

Without explicitly referring to homosexuality, the executive order responded to several years of charges that the presence of homosexual employees in the State Department posed blackmail risks. Attorney General [Herbert Brownell Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Brownell_Jr.) explained that the new order was designed to encompass both loyalty and security risks and he differentiated between the two: "Employees could be a security risk and still not be disloyal or have any traitorous thoughts, but it may be that their personal habits are such that they might be subject to blackmail by people who seek to destroy the safety of our country."[^cite_note-lavender-429]

The press recognized the revolutionary nature of the new executive order. *The Washington Post* said that it established not a loyalty test but a "suitability test." Some in government referred to their new "integrity-security" program. Some of those the press expected to be excluded from federal employment included "a person who drinks too much," "an incorrigible gossip," "homosexuals," and "neurotics."[^cite_note-lavender-429]

In 1953, Congress changed the solicitation law in the District of Columbia so that the jail term of up to 90 days was retained, but the maximum fine was raised to \$250, and the reference to the power of judges to "impose conditions" on the defendant was removed. The law went into effect on June 29, 1953.[^cite_note-DC_Sodomy_law-418]

### John F. Kennedy



Letter from Kameny to President Kennedy, JFK Library

In late 1961, [Frank Kameny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Kameny) co-founded the Washington D.C. branch of the national gay rights organization, [Mattachine Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mattachine_Society). In the year following the group's founding, Kameny led an initiative to declare the existence of the Mattachine Society of Washington publicly. The group sent letters to every government branch, including the entirety of Congress.[^cite_note-DW-430] Kameny also wrote to President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) asking him to change the rules on homosexuals being purged from the government.[^cite_note-431] The content of the letters included harsh criticism of the government's treatment of homosexuals and asserted that there were over three hundred members of the group. The letter was signed by the president of the Mattachine Society of Washington, Franklin E. Kameny.[^cite_note-DW-430]

The Mattachine Society of Washington discussed the prospect of public protest in 1963. The FBI and J. Edgar Hoover had made advancements to ban the Washington branch and had been threatened with the prospect of a march on behalf of the organization.[^cite_note-432] Although supportive of the idea, Kameny restrained from taking part in a march due to the threat of damaging his public image.[^cite_note-433]

In 1963, Kameny and Mattachine launched a campaign to overturn D.C. [sodomy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sodomy_laws).

### Lyndon B. Johnson

#### Senator of Texas

On February 2, 1950, Senator [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) voted for [Uniform Code of Military Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniform_Code_of_Military_Justice).[^cite_note-434]

#### Presidency

On October 19, 1964, [Walter Jenkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Jenkins), a longtime top aide to President Johnson, had been arrested by [District of Columbia Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Department_of_the_District_of_Columbia) in a [YMCA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YMCA) restroom. He and another man were booked on a disorderly conduct charge.[^cite_note-435]

After becoming a controversy prior to the [1964 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election), the American Mental Health Foundation wrote a letter to President Johnson protesting the "hysteria" surrounding the case:[^cite_note-436]

The private life and inclinations of a citizen, Government employee or not, does not necessarily have any bearing on his capacities, usefulness, and sense of responsibility in his occupation. The fact that an individual is homosexual, as has been strongly implied in the case of Mr. Jenkins, does not per se make him more unstable and more a security risk than any heterosexual person.

After reelection during his second term on October 3, 1965, Johnson signed the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1965), which added "sexual deviation" as a medical ground for denying prospective immigrants entry into the United States. The bill went into effect on June 30, 1968.[^cite_note-Sexual_Orientation_and_Asylum_in_the_United_States-422]

### Richard Nixon



The [Stonewall Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_Inn) in the [gay village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_village) of [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village), [Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_culture_in_New_York_City), adorned with [rainbow flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_flag_\(LGBTQ_movement\)) during a [pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_pride) event. The Inn was the site of the eponymous [Stonewall riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_riots) in June 1969: a series of events which precipitated the modern [LGBTQ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights) movement. Stonewall has since become an icon of [LGBTQ culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_culture) and [gay pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_pride) in the United States.

[^cite_note-GayGreenwichVillage1-437]

[^cite_note-StonewallNationalMonument1-438]

[^cite_note-GayGreenwichVillage3-439]

#### Presidency

In August 1970, [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), on the issue of same-sex marriage, said "I can't go that far; that's the year 2000! Negroes and whites, okay. But that's too far!"[^cite_note-440]

In 1972, San Francisco's Gay Activists Alliance disbanded and formed the Gay Voters League, a group that campaigned for the reelection of President Richard Nixon.[^cite_note-Republican_Party_(United_States)_gay_rights-441] In October 1972, a representative of the Committee to Re-elect the President addressed gay voters on behalf of Nixon's campaign in San Francisco. The event was organized by the Gay Voters League of San Francisco.[^cite_note-442]

### Gerald Ford

#### House Minority Leader

On August 25, 1965, Rep. [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) voted for the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1965).[^cite_note-443]

#### Presidency

On March 5, 1976, when asked about the issue of gay rights, with respect to hiring, employment, and housing, [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) said "I recognize that this is a very new and serious problem in our society. I have always tried to be an understanding person as far as people are concerned who are different than myself. That doesn't mean that I agree with or would concur in what is done by them or their position in society. I think this is a problem we have to face up to, and I can't give you a pat answer tonight. I just would be dishonest to say that there is a pat answer under these very difficult circumstances".[^cite_note-444]

In 1976, during that year's presidential campaign, President Gerald Ford was "[zapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zap_\(action\))" by activists in [Ann Arbor, Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Arbor,_Michigan) over federal immigration rules. The protests forced President Ford to admit that he was not aware that homosexuality was used as a basis for exclusion in immigration rulings.[^cite_note-Republican_Party_(United_States)_gay_rights-441]

#### Post-presidency

[Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford), as former president, formally opposed the [Briggs Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briggs_Initiative) in 1977, which sought to ban homosexuals from teaching in public school. In October 2001, he broke with conservative members of the Republican Party by stating that gay and lesbian couples "ought to be treated equally. Period." He became the highest ranking Republican to embrace full equality for gays and lesbians, stating his belief that there should be a federal amendment outlawing anti-gay job discrimination and expressing his hope that the Republican Party would reach out to gay and lesbian voters.[^cite_note-445] He also was a member of the [Republican Unity Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Unity_Coalition), which *[The New York Times](30680.md)* described as "a group of prominent Republicans, including former President Gerald R. Ford, dedicated to making sexual orientation a non-issue in the Republican Party".[^cite_note-446]

### Jimmy Carter

#### Post-governorship of Georgia

In February 1976, Carter said he opposed discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation, but in June 1976 he withdrew his support of a gay rights plank in the Democratic Party platform.[^cite_note-gay_rights_2002-447]

#### Presidency

In 1977, under the guidance of Jimmy Carter, a policy was removed which barred employment of gays in the [foreign service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_service) and [Internal Revenue Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service). That same year, fourteen gay and lesbian activists were invited to the White House for the first official visit ever. Jimmy Carter publicly opposed the [Briggs Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briggs_Initiative). However, in March 1980, Carter issued a formal statement indicating he would not issue an executive order banning anti-gay discrimination in the U.S. federal government and that he would not support including a gay rights plank in the Democratic Party platform.[^cite_note-gay_rights_2002-447][^cite_note-448] In September 1980, the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) announced that immigration officials would no longer be allowed to ask whether an individual entering the United States was gay and therefore ineligible for admission.[^cite_note-nunes-449] An individual would only be denied admission into the United States if the traveler self-identified as gay to the immigration official.[^cite_note-nunes-449]

#### Post-presidency

In 2004, Carter came out for [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_unions) and stated that he "opposes all forms of discrimination on the basis of [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) and believes there should be [equal protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_protection) under the law for people who differ in sexual orientation".[^cite_note-450] In 2007, he called for ending the ban on [gays in the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gays_in_the_military).[^cite_note-451] In March 2012, Jimmy Carter came out in favor of same sex marriage.[^cite_note-Carter-452]

### Ronald Reagan

#### Post-governorship of California

The first chapter of what would become the national [Log Cabin Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_Cabin_Republicans) (LCR) formed in 1978 to fight California's [Briggs Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briggs_Initiative), a ballot initiative that would have banned homosexuals from teaching in public schools. The chapter worked diligently and successfully convinced Governor Reagan to publicly oppose the measure.[^cite_note-Republican_Party_(United_States)_gay_rights-441] Reagan penned an op-ed against the Briggs Initiative in which he wrote, "Whatever else it is, homosexuality is not a contagious disease like the measles. Prevailing scientific opinion is that an individual's sexuality is determined at a very early age and that a child's teachers do not really influence this."[^cite_note-gay_lovers_wh-453]

#### Presidency

Main article: [Domestic policy of the Ronald Reagan administration § LGBTQ Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration#LGBTQ_Rights)

On the [1980 campaign trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election), he spoke of the gay civil rights movement:

> My criticism is that \[the gay movement] isn't just asking for civil rights; it's asking for recognition and acceptance of an alternative lifestyle which I do not believe society can condone, nor can I.[^cite_note-454]

No civil rights legislation for LGBTQ individuals passed during Reagan's tenure. Additionally, Reagan has been criticized for ignoring (by failing to adequately address or fund) the growing [AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_epidemic), even as it took thousands of lives in the 1980s. Reagan's Surgeon General from 1982 to 1989, Dr. [C. Everett Koop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Everett_Koop), claims that his attempts to address the issue were shut out by the Reagan administration. According to Koop, the prevailing view of the Reagan administration was that "transmission of AIDS was understood to be primarily in the homosexual population and in those who abused intravenous drugs" and therefore that people dying from AIDS were "only getting what they justly deserve."[^cite_note-455]

On August 18, 1984, President Reagan issued a statement on the issue of same-sex marriage that read:

> Society has always regarded marital love as a sacred expression of the bond between a man and a woman. It is the means by which families are created and society itself is extended into the future. In the Judeo-Christian tradition it is the means by which husband and wife participate with God in the creation of a new human life. It is for these reasons, among others, that our society has always sought to protect this unique relationship. In part the erosion of these values has given way to a celebration of forms of expression most reject. We will resist the efforts of some to obtain government endorsement of homosexuality.

Reagan made the comment in response to a questionnaire from the conservative publishers of the Presidential Biblical Scoreboard, a magazine-type compilation of past statements and voting records of national candidates.[^cite_note-456]

### George H. W. Bush

#### Vice presidency

In 1988, the Republican Party's nominee, Vice President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), endorsed a plan to protect persons with AIDS from discrimination.[^cite_note-Republican_Party_(United_States)_gay_rights-441]

#### Presidency

As President, George H. W. Bush signed legislation that extended gay rights. On April 23, 1990, George H. W. Bush signed the [Hate Crime Statistics Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_Crime_Statistics_Act), which requires the [Attorney General](44002.md) to collect data on crimes committed because of the victim's race, religion, disability, sexual orientation, or ethnicity. It was the first federal statute to "recognize and name gay, lesbian and bisexual people."[^cite_note-457] On July 26, 1990, George H. W. Bush signed the [Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_with_Disabilities_Act_of_1990). On November 29, 1990, Bush signed the [Immigration Act of 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1990), which withdrew the phrase "sexual deviation" from the Immigration and Nationality Act (INA) so that it could no longer be used as a basis for barring entry of immigration to the U.S. for homosexuals.[^cite_note-Republican_Party_(United_States)_gay_rights-441]

In a television interview, Bush said if he found out his grandchild was gay, he would "love his child", but tell him homosexuality was not normal and discourage him from working for gay rights. In February 1992, the chairman of the Bush-Quayle campaign met with the National Gay and Lesbian Task Force.[^cite_note-gay_rights_2002-447] In May 1992, he appointed Anne-Imelda Radice to serve as the Acting Chairman of the National Endowment for the Arts.[^cite_note-458] Losing ground in the 1992 Republican president primary to President Bush's far-right challenger, [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan), the Bush campaign turned to the right, and President Bush publicly denounced same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-459]

The 1992 Log Cabin Republican convention was held in [Spring, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spring,_Texas), a [Houston](13774.md) [exurb](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/exurb). The main issue discussed was whether or not LCR would endorse the re-election of President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush). The group voted to deny that endorsement because Bush did not denounce [anti-gay rhetoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-gay_rhetoric) at the [1992 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Republican_National_Convention).[^cite_note-trib-460] Many in the gay community believed President Bush had not done enough on the issue of AIDS. [Urvashi Vaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urvashi_Vaid) argues that Bush's anti-gay rhetoric "motivated conservative gay Democrats and loyal gay Republicans, who had helped defeat Dukakis in 1988, to throw their support behind Clinton."[^cite_note-gay_rights_2002-447]

In 1992, the [Council of the District of Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_District_of_Columbia) passed the "Health Benefits Expansion Act", which was signed into law by the [Mayor of Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Washington,_D.C.) The bill, which established [domestic partnerships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_partnerships) in the District of Columbia, became law on June 11, 1992. Every year from 1992 to 2000, the Republican leadership of the U.S. Congress added a [rider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rider_\(legislation\)) to the District of Columbia [appropriations bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appropriation_bill) that prohibited the use of federal or local funds to implement the Health Care Benefits Expansion Act.[^cite_note-citizenlink.org-461] On October 5, 1992, Bush signed the H.R. 6056 into law, which included the Republican rider to the appropriations bill.[^cite_note-462]

#### Post-presidency

In 2013, former President George H. W. Bush served as a witness at a same-sex wedding of Bonnie Clement and Helen Thorgalsen, who own a general store together in Maine.[^cite_note-463] In 2015, *[The Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boston_Globe)* reported that Bush "offered to perform the ceremony but had a scheduling conflict."[^cite_note-464]

### Bill Clinton

#### Governorship of Arkansas

In 1992, Governor Bill Clinton, as a candidate for president, issued a public statement of support for repeal of Arkansas's sodomy law.[^cite_note-465] Also in 1992, the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign), America's largest LGBTQ rights organization, issued its first presidential endorsement in 1992 to Bill Clinton.[^cite_note-newyorker.com-466]

#### Presidency

[Bill Clinton](3356.md)'s legacy on gay rights is a matter of controversy. LGBTQ rights activist [Richard Socarides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Socarides) credits Clinton as the first president to publicly champion gay rights,[^cite_note-newyorker.com-466] but Clinton's signing of DOMA and DADT have led critics like [Andrew Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Sullivan) to argue Clinton was a detriment to rather than an ally for the LGBTQ rights movement, though DOMA passed Congress with veto-proof majorities in the House and Senate.[^cite_note-467] In December 1993, Clinton implemented a Department of Defense directive known as "[Don't Ask, Don't Tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell)", which allowed [gay men and women](5488304.md) to serve in the armed services provided they kept their sexuality a secret, and forbade the military from inquiring about an individual's sexual orientation.[^cite_note-468] The policy was developed as a compromise after Clinton's proposal to allow gays to serve openly in the military met with staunch opposition from prominent congressional Republicans and Democrats, including Senators [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) (R-AZ) and [Sam Nunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Nunn) (D-GA). According to [David Mixner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Mixner), Clinton's support for the compromise led to a heated dispute with Vice President [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), who felt that "the President should lift the ban ... even though \[his executive order] was sure to be overridden by the Congress".[^cite_note-469]

Some gay-rights advocates criticized Clinton for not going far enough and accused him of making his campaign promise to get votes and contributions.[^cite_note-470] Their position was that Clinton should have integrated the military by executive order, noting that President [Harry Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Truman) used executive order to racially desegregate the armed forces. Clinton's defenders argue that an executive order might have prompted the Senate to write the exclusion of gays into law, potentially making it harder to integrate the military in the future.[^cite_note-The_Natural-471] Later in his presidency, in 1999, Clinton criticized the way the policy was implemented, saying he did not think any serious person could say it was not "out of whack".[^cite_note-472] On September 21, 1996, Clinton signed into law the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) (DOMA), which defines marriage for federal purposes as the legal union of one man and one woman, allowing individual states to refuse to recognize gay marriages performed in other states.[^cite_note-473] [Paul Yandura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Yandura), speaking for the White House gay and lesbian liaison office, said that Clinton's signing of DOMA "was a political decision that they made at the time of a re-election." In defense of his actions, Clinton has said that DOMA was an attempt to "head off an attempt to send a constitutional amendment banning gay marriage to the states", a possibility he described as highly likely in the context of a "very reactionary Congress."[^cite_note-474]

Administration spokesman [Richard Socarides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Socarides) said, "... the alternatives we knew were going to be far worse, and it was time to move on and get the president re-elected."[^cite_note-metroweekly-475] Others were more critical. The veteran gay rights and gay marriage activist [Evan Wolfson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Wolfson) has called these claims "historic revisionism".[^cite_note-metroweekly-475] In a July 2, 2011, editorial *[The New York Times](30680.md)* opined, "The Defense of Marriage Act was enacted in 1996 as an election-year wedge issue, signed by President Bill Clinton in one of his worst policy moments."[^cite_note-476] Despite DOMA, Clinton, who was the first president to select openly gay persons for administration positions,[^cite_note-477] is generally credited as the first president to publicly champion gay rights.[^cite_note-newyorker.com-466] During his presidency, Clinton controversially issued two substantial executive orders on behalf of gay rights, the first was [Executive Order 12968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_12968) in 1995 that lifted the ban on security clearances for LGBTQ federal employees[^cite_note-478] and the second was [Executive Order 13087](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13087) in 1998 that outlawed discrimination based on sexual orientation in the federal civilian workforce.[^cite_note-479] In November 1997, Clinton gave an address to a [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign) meeting, and thus becoming the first U.S. President to address a meeting for a gay and lesbian organization.[^cite_note-480]

Under President Clinton's leadership, federal funding for HIV/AIDS research, prevention and treatment more than doubled.[^cite_note-481] And Clinton also pushed for passing hate crimes laws for gays and for the private sector [Employment Non-Discrimination Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Non-Discrimination_Act), which, buoyed by his lobbying, failed to pass the Senate by a single vote in 1996.[^cite_note-482] Advocacy for these issues, paired with the politically unpopular nature of the gay rights movement at the time, led to enthusiastic support for Clinton's reelection in 1996 by the Human Rights Campaign.[^cite_note-newyorker.com-466] Clinton was the first president to select openly gay persons for administration positions, appointing over 150 LGBTQ appointees.[^cite_note-483] The first openly gay U.S. ambassador, [James Hormel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hormel), received a [recess appointment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recess_appointment) from the President after the Senate failed to confirm the nomination. On June 11, 1999, Clinton declared June to be Gay and Lesbian [Pride Month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_Month), making him the first Democratic president to do so.[^cite_note-usgovinfo.about.com-484]

#### Post-presidency

In 2008, Clinton publicly opposed the passage of California's [Proposition 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposition_8) and recorded robocalls urging Californians to vote against it.[^cite_note-485] In July 2009, he came out in favor of same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-486] On March 7, 2013, Clinton called for the overturn of the Defense of Marriage Act by the U.S. Supreme Court.[^cite_note-487]

### George W. Bush

In his 1994 campaign to become the [Governor of Texas](214883.md), Bush pledged to veto any effort to repeal Texas's sodomy law, calling it "a symbolic gesture of traditional values."[^cite_note-Byrd_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Act-488]

#### Governor of Texas

In 1997, Governor Bush signed into law a bill adding "A license may not be issued for the marriage of persons of the same sex" into the Texas Family Code.[^cite_note-489] In a 1998 Texas Gubernatorial election political awareness test, he answered no to the questions of whether Texas government should include sexual orientation in Texas' anti-discrimination laws and whether he supports Texas recognizing same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-490]

In 1999, the Byrd Jr. Hate Crimes Act, which would have increased punishment for criminals motivated by hatred of a victim's gender, religion, ethnic background or sexual orientation, was killed in committee by Texas Senate Republicans. Governor Bush was criticized for letting the hate crimes bill die in a Texas Senate committee. Bush spokesman Sullivan said the governor never took a position on the bill. According to Louvon Harris, sister of James Byrd, said that Bush's opposition to the bill reportedly revolved around the fact that it would cover gays and lesbians.[^cite_note-Byrd_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Act-488]

The governor's office "contacted the family and asked if we would consider taking sexual orientation out of the bill, and our answer was no, because the bill is for everybody. Everybody should be protected by the law." said Harris. In a 2000 presidential debate, [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) would attack Bush for allowing the bill to die in committee, with Bush responding Texas already had a hate crimes statute, and nothing more was needed.[^cite_note-Byrd_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Act-488] George W. Bush also stated his opposition to a New Jersey Supreme Court ruling that said the [Boy Scouts of America must accept gays in their organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boy_Scouts_of_America_membership_controversies). "I believe the Boy Scouts is a private organization and they should be able to set the standards that they choose to set," Bush said.[^cite_note-Bush_civil_rights-491] Bush also expressed his support for bans on gay foster parenting and adoption,[^cite_note-492] urging agencies to place children in "traditional homes—man and wife."[^cite_note-493]

During the 2000 campaign he did not endorse a single piece of [gay rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_rights) legislation. In a 2000 Republican presidential debate, George W. Bush said he opposes same-sex marriage, but supports [states' rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States'_rights) when it came to the issue of same-sex marriage. During the campaign he had refused to comment on Vermont's civil unions law.[^cite_note-Bush_civil_rights-491] On April 13, 2000, Governor Bush became first presumptive GOP presidential nominee ever to meet publicly with gay Republicans in Austin, Texas.[^cite_note-494]

On August 4, 2000, Bush received the endorsement of the [Log Cabin Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_Cabin_Republicans), the GOP's largest gay group, for president.[^cite_note-Gay_Republicans_Endorse_Bush-495] He also received the endorsement of the newly formed [Republican Unity Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Unity_Coalition).[^cite_note-Gay_Republicans_Endorse_Bush-495][^cite_note-496] In a 2000 presidential debate with Al Gore, Bush stated he supported the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) and the "[Don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell)" policy. However, he stated that he opposed sodomy laws, a reversal of his position as governor of Texas.[^cite_note-Bush_civil_rights-491][^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498]

#### Presidency

Main articles: [Domestic policy of the George W. Bush administration § LGBT_issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_George_W._Bush_administration#LGBT_issues), and [Political positions of George W. Bush § LGBT_issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_George_W._Bush#LGBT_issues)

[George W. Bush](3414021.md), despite being opposed to LGBTQ rights when Governor of Texas, was relatively moderate in regards to LGBTQ rights as president, though opposed gay marriage and would later voice his support for civil unions. In his eight years of office, Bush's views on [gay rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_rights) were often difficult to ascertain, but many experts feel that the Bush White House wanted to avoid bad publicity without alienating evangelical conservative Christian voters. Thus, he did not repeal President Clinton's Executive Order banning discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation in the federal civilian government, but Bush's critics felt as if he failed to enforce the executive order.[^cite_note-499] He retained Clinton's Office of National AIDS Policy and was the first Republican president to appoint an openly gay man to serve in his administration, [Scott Evertz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Evertz) as director of the [Office of National AIDS Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_National_AIDS_Policy).[^cite_note-500] Bush also became the second president, after Clinton, to select openly gay appointees to his administration. Bush's nominee as ambassador to Romania, [Michael E. Guest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._Guest), became the second openly gay man U.S. Ambassador and the first to be confirmed by the Senate. He did not repeal any of the spousal benefits that Clinton had introduced for same-sex federal employees. He did not attempt to repeal [don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell), nor make an effort to change it.[^cite_note-Bush_civil_rights-491]

In April 2002, White House officials held an unannounced briefing in April for the Log Cabin Republicans. On June 27, 2002, President Bush signed a bill allowing death benefits to be paid to [domestic partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_partner) of firefighters and police officers who die in the line of duty, permanently extending a federal death benefit to same-sex couples for the first time.[^cite_note-501] In 2003, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled in *[Lawrence v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_v._Texas)* that sodomy laws against consenting adults was unconstitutional. President Bush's press secretary [Ari Fleischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ari_Fleischer) refused to comment on the decision, noting only that the administration had not filed a brief in the case.[^cite_note-502] In 2004, Bush said "What they do in the privacy of their house, consenting adults should be able to do."[^cite_note-503]

Previously, Bush said he supports states' rights when it came to marriage, however, after the [Massachusetts](1645518.md) Supreme Court decision in *[Goodridge v. Department of Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodridge_v._Department_of_Public_Health)*, Bush announced his support for a U.S. constitutional amendment banning same-sex marriage on February 24, 2004.[^cite_note-504] Due to his support of the [Federal Marriage Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Marriage_Amendment) (FMA), the [Log Cabin Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_Cabin_Republicans) declined to endorse the reelection of [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in 2004[^cite_note-trib-460] by a vote of 22–2.[^cite_note-505] The Palm Beach County chapter in Florida did endorse him, resulting in the revocation of their charter.[^cite_note-wapo-506] On September 22, 2004, the Abe Lincoln Black Republican Caucus (ALBRC), a group of young urban Black gay Republicans, voted in a special call meeting in Dallas, Texas, to endorse President Bush for re-election.[^cite_note-507] In an October president debate, Bush said he did not know whether homosexuality is a choice or not.[^cite_note-Bush_civil_rights-491] In October 2004, Bush said that he supported allowing the states to establish [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_union) for same-sex couples.[^cite_note-508]

In 2007, Bush threatened to veto the [Local Law Enforcement Hate Crimes Prevention Act of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Shepard_Act), which would have included [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation) in hate crimes, and [Employment Nondiscrimination Act of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Nondiscrimination_Act). In December 2008, the Bush administration refused to support the U.N. declaration on [sexual orientation and gender identity at the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation_and_gender_identity_at_the_United_Nations) that condemns the use of violence, harassment, discrimination, exclusion, stigmatization, and prejudice based on sexual orientation and gender identity.[^cite_note-509]

### Barack Obama

#### Illinois state senator

[Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) supported legalizing [same-sex marriage](92656.md) when he first ran for the Illinois State Senate in 1996.[^cite_note-Baim_2009a-510] When he ran for re-election to the Illinois Senate in 1998, he was undecided about legalizing same-sex marriage and supported including sexual orientation to the state's non-discrimination laws.[^cite_note-Baim_2009b-511][^cite_note-Obama_civil_rights-512] During his time as a state senator he cosponsored a bill amending the Illinois Human Rights Act to include protections for LGBTQ people which prohibits discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation and gender identity in the workplace, housing, and all public places and supported Illinois gender violence act.

#### U.S. Senator from Illinois

Obama supported [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_union), but opposed same-sex marriage when he ran for the U.S. Senate in 2004 and for U.S. President in 2008.[^cite_note-Baim_2009a-510] He supported civil unions that would carry equal legal standing to that of marriage for [same-sex couples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_couples), but believed that decisions about the title of marriage should be left to the states.[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514]

During his time as senator, Obama co-sponsored the [Employment Non-Discrimination Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Non-Discrimination_Act), [Matthew Shepard Local Law Enforcement Hate Crimes Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Shepard_and_James_Byrd,_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Prevention_Act), Tax Equity for Domestic Partner and Health Plan Beneficiaries Act, and Early Treatment for HIV Act.[^cite_note-110th_congress_Obama-515][^cite_note-Obama_scorecard-516]

In the [109th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/109th_United_States_Congress), Obama received a score of 89% by the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign).[^cite_note-Obama_scorecard-516]

In 2006, Obama voted against the [Federal Marriage Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Marriage_Amendment), which would have defined marriage as between one man and one woman in the U.S. Constitution.[^cite_note-517]

In 2007, Senator Obama said he opposed the 1996 [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) and the [don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell) policy when it passed and supported repealing it. He also said that homosexuality is not a choice, he supported adoption rights for same-sex couples, and he would work as president to extend the 1,000 federal rights granted to marriage couples to couples in civil unions.
