He also voted for the Kennedy Amendment to the [National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2008) that would expand federal jurisdiction to reach serious, violent hate crimes perpetrated because of the victim's sexual orientation and gender identity and the Tom Lantos and Henry J. Hyde United States Global Leadership Against HIV/AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria Reauthorization Act.[^cite_note-Obama_civil_rights-512][^cite_note-110th_congress_Obama-515]

In the 2008 presidential election, he expressed his opposition to state constitutional bans on same-sex marriage in [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposition_8), and [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Florida_Amendment_2) on the November ballot,[^cite_note-518][^cite_note-519][^cite_note-520] but stated in a 2008 interview that he personally believes that marriage is "between a man and a woman" and that he is "not in favor of [gay marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_marriage)."[^cite_note-521] In the [110th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/110th_United_States_Congress), Obama received a score of 94% by the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign).[^cite_note-Obama_scorecard-516] In the 2008 election, Obama received the endorsement of the following gay rights organizations: [Houston GLBT Political Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_GLBT_Political_Caucus),[^cite_note-Bloomberg1-522] [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign),[^cite_note-523] and the [National Stonewall Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stonewall_Democrats).[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-BaimBesen2010-525][^cite_note-526]

#### Presidency

Main article: [Social policy of the Barack Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_policy_of_the_Barack_Obama_administration)

##### First term

Barack Obama took many definitively pro-LGBTQ stances. In March 2009, his administration reversed Bush administration policy and signed the [U.N. declaration that calls for the decriminalization of homosexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_at_the_United_Nations).[^cite_note-527] In June 2009, Obama became the first president to declare the month of June to be LGBTQ pride month; Bill Clinton had declared June Gay and Lesbian Pride Month. Obama would do the same for every following June of his presidency.[^cite_note-usgovinfo.about.com-484][^cite_note-528][^cite_note-529][^cite_note-530][^cite_note-531][^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534][^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536] On June 17, 2009, Obama signed a [presidential memorandum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_memorandum) allowing same-sex partners of federal employees to receive certain benefits. The memorandum does not cover full health coverage.[^cite_note-537] On October 28, 2009, Obama signed the [Matthew Shepard and James Byrd Jr. Hate Crimes Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Shepard_and_James_Byrd_Jr._Hate_Crimes_Prevention_Act), which added [gender](38076.md), [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation), [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity), and [disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability) to the federal hate crimes law.[^cite_note-538] In October 2009, he nominated [Sharon Lubinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharon_Lubinski) to become the first openly gay [U.S. marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshal) to serve the Minnesota district.[^cite_note-539]

On January 4, 2010, he appointed [Amanda Simpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Simpson) the Senior Technical Advisor to the [Department of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Commerce), making her the first openly transgender person appointed to a government post by a U.S. President.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541][^cite_note-542] He has appointed the most U.S. gay and lesbian officials of any U.S. president.[^cite_note-543] At the start of 2010, the Obama administration included gender identity among the classes protected against discrimination under the authority of the [Equal Employment Opportunity Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Employment_Opportunity_Commission) (EEOC). On April 15, 2010, Obama issued an executive order to the Department of Health and Human Services that required medical facilities to grant visitation and medical decision-making rights to same-sex couples.[^cite_note-544] In June 2010, he expanded the Family Medical Leave Act to cover employees taking unpaid leave to care for the children of same-sex partners.[^cite_note-545] On December 22, 2010, Obama signed the [Don't Ask, Don't Tell Repeal Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell_Repeal_Act_of_2010) into law.[^cite_note-546]

On February 23, 2011, Obama instructed the Justice Department to stop defending the Defense of Marriage Act in court.[^cite_note-547] In March 2011, the U.S. issued a nonbinding declaration in favor of gay rights that gained the support of more than 80 countries at the U.N.[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-548] In June 2011, the U.N. endorsed the rights of gay, lesbian, and transgender people for the first time, by passing a resolution that was backed by the U.S., among other countries.[^cite_note-huffingtonpost.com-548] On August 18, 2011, the Obama administration announced that it would suspend deportation proceedings against many undocumented immigrants who pose no threat to national security or public safety, with the White House interpreting the term "family" to include partners of lesbian, gay and bisexual people.[^cite_note-549]

On September 30, 2011, the [Defense Department](7279897.md) issued new guidelines that allow military chaplains to officiate at same-sex weddings, on or off military installations, in states where such weddings are allowed.[^cite_note-550] On December 5, 2011, the Obama administration announced the United States would use all the tools of American diplomacy, including the potent enticement of foreign aid, to promote LGBTQ rights around the world.[^cite_note-551] In March and April 2012, Obama expressed his opposition to state constitutional bans on same-sex marriage in [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Amendment_1), and [Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota_Amendment_1).[^cite_note-552] On May 3, 2012, the [Federal Bureau of Prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Prisons) has agreed to add an LGBTQ representative to the diversity program at each of the 120 prisons it operates in the United States.[^cite_note-553]

On May 9, 2012, Obama publicly supported same-sex marriage, the first sitting U.S. President to do so. Obama told an interviewer that:[^cite_note-554]

> over the course of several years as I have talked to friends and family and neighbors when I think about members of my own staff who are in incredibly committed monogamous relationships, same-sex relationships, who are raising kids together, when I think about those soldiers or airmen or Marines or sailors who are out there fighting on my behalf and yet feel constrained, even now that Don't Ask Don't Tell is gone, because they are not able to commit themselves in a marriage, at a certain point I've just concluded that for me personally it is important for me to go ahead and affirm that I think same sex couples should be able to get married.

In the 2012 election, Obama received the endorsement of the following gay rights organizations: Equal Rights Washington, Fair Wisconsin, [Gay-Straight Alliance Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay-Straight_Alliance_Network),[^cite_note-555][^cite_note-556] [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign),[^cite_note-557] and the [National Stonewall Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Stonewall_Democrats). The [American Civil Liberties Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_Liberties_Union) (ACLU) gave Obama a score of 100% on the issue of gays and lesbians in the U.S. military and a score of 75% on the issue of freedom to marry for gay people.[^cite_note-558]

##### Second term



On September 3, 2013, same-sex spouses of Service members were eligible for military benefits.

On January 7, 2013, the Pentagon agreed to pay full separation pay to service members discharged under "[Don't Ask, Don't Tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell)."[^cite_note-559] Obama also called for full equality during his second [inaugural address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inaugural_address) on January 21, 2013: "Our journey is not complete until our gay brothers and sisters are treated like anyone else under the law—for if we are truly created equal, then surely the love we commit to one another must be equal as well." It was the first mention of rights for gays and lesbians or use of the word *gay* in an inaugural address.[^cite_note-560][^cite_note-561]

On March 1, 2013, Obama, speaking about *[Hollingsworth v. Perry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollingsworth_v._Perry)*, the U.S. Supreme Court case about [Proposition 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposition_8), said "When the Supreme Court asks do you think that the California law, which doesn't provide any rationale for discriminating against same-sex couples other than just the notion that, well, they're same-sex couples—if the Supreme Court asks me or my attorney general or solicitor general, 'Do we think that meets constitutional muster?' I felt it was important for us to answer that question honestly. And the answer is no." The administration took the position that the Supreme Court should apply "heightened scrutiny" to California's ban—a standard under which legal experts say no state ban could survive.[^cite_note-562]

On August 7, 2013, Obama criticized the [Russian gay propaganda law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_gay_propaganda_law).[^cite_note-563] On December 26, 2013, Obama signed the [National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Authorization_Act_for_Fiscal_Year_2014) into law, which repealed the ban on consensual sodomy in the [UCMJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniform_Code_of_Military_Justice).[^cite_note-564] On February 16, 2014, Obama criticized the [Uganda Anti-Homosexuality Act, 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uganda_Anti-Homosexuality_Act,_2014).[^cite_note-565] On February 28, 2014, Obama agreed with the [Governor of Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Arizona) [Jan Brewer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Brewer)'s veto of [SB 1062](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SB_1062).[^cite_note-566] Obama included openly gay athletes in the [2014 Olympic delegation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Winter_Olympics), namely [Brian Boitano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Boitano) and [Billie Jean King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Jean_King) (who was later replaced by [Caitlin Cahow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caitlin_Cahow)).[^cite_note-567][^cite_note-usatoday.com-568] This was done in criticism of Russia's anti-gay law.[^cite_note-usatoday.com-568] On July 21, 2014, Obama signed [Executive Order 13672](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13672), adding "gender identity" to the categories protected against discrimination in hiring in the federal civilian workforce and both "sexual orientation" and "gender identity" to the categories protected against discrimination in hiring and employment on the part of federal government contractors and sub-contractors.[^cite_note-The_White_House-76] Obama was criticized for meeting with anti-gay Ugandan president [Yoweri Museveni](293409.md) at a dinner with African heads of state in August 2014.[^cite_note-569]

Later in August 2014, Obama made a surprise video appearance at the opening ceremony of the 2014 [Gay Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_Games).[^cite_note-570][^cite_note-571] On February 10, 2015, [David Axelrod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Axelrod)'s *Believer: My Forty Years in Politics* was published. In the book, Axelrod revealed that Obama lied about his opposition to same-sex marriage for religious reasons in [2008 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election). "I'm just not very good at bullshitting," Obama told Axelrod, after an event where he stated his opposition to same-sex marriage, according to the book.[^cite_note-572] In 2015, the U.S. appointed [Randy Berry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Berry) as its first Special Envoy for the Human Rights of LGBTQ Persons.[^cite_note-573]

In April 2015, the Obama administration announced it had opened a gender-neutral bathroom within the White House complex, located in the [Eisenhower Executive Office Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisenhower_Executive_Office_Building) next door to the [West Wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Wing).[^cite_note-574] Obama also responded to a petition seeking to ban [conversion therapy](26410.md) (inspired by the death of [Leelah Alcorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leelah_Alcorn)) with his pledge to advocate for such a ban.[^cite_note-575] Also in 2015, when Obama declared May to be [National Foster Care Month](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Foster_Care_Month), he included words never before included in a White House proclamation about adoption, stating in part, "With so many children waiting for loving homes, it is important to ensure all qualified caregivers have the opportunity to serve as foster or adoptive parents, regardless of race, religion, sexual orientation, gender identity, or marital status. That is why we are working to break down the barriers that exist and investing in efforts to recruit more qualified parents for children in foster care." He was the first president to explicitly say [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) should not prevent anyone from adopting or becoming a foster parent.[^cite_note-576]

On October 29, 2015, Obama endorsed [Proposition 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston,_Texas_Proposition_1,_2015) in Houston, Texas.[^cite_note-577] On November 10, 2015, Obama officially announced his support for the [Equality Act of 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_of_2015).[^cite_note-578] In June 2016, Obama and Vice President [Joe Biden](145422.md) met with the victims and families of victims of the [Orlando nightclub shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_nightclub_shooting). Obama and Biden laid 49 bouquets of white roses to memorialize the 49 people killed in the tragedy impacting the LGBTQ community.[^cite_note-579] On June 24, 2016, Obama designated the [Stonewall National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stonewall_National_Monument) in [Greenwich Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Village), [Lower Manhattan](455646.md), as the first [national monument in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Monument_\(United_States\)) to honor the LGBTQ rights movement.[^cite_note-StonewallNationalMonument1-438] On October 20, 2016, Obama endorsed [Kate Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Brown) as Governor of [Oregon](26811621.md).[^cite_note-580] On November 8, Brown became the first openly LGBTQ person to be elected governor in the United States. Brown is a [bisexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisexual) woman who has also come out as a survivor of [sexual assault](218407.md) and [domestic violence](18978563.md).[^cite_note-581][^cite_note-582] Before being elected in her own right, Brown had assumed the governorship due to a resignation. During that time, she signed legislation to ban [conversion therapy](26410.md) on minors.[^cite_note-583]

### Donald Trump

See also: [Social policy of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_policy_of_Donald_Trump)

[Donald Trump](4848272.md) opposed expanding LGBTQ rights.[^cite_note-584] As President, he rolled back LGBTQ rights and appointed anti-LGBTQ officials, but also gay officials such as [Scott Bessent](38532044.md) as [Secretary of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Treasury) and [Richard Grenell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Grenell) as [special presidential envoy for special missions](153068.md#Special_envoys,_representatives,_and_coordinators).[^cite_note-585] He opposed the [Equality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-586] which has been one of the highest priorities of LGBTQ rights groups since same-sex marriage was enacted by the Supreme Court.[^cite_note-587] Long before his 2016 campaign, Trump opposed the legalization of same-sex marriage;[^cite_note-588] during his 2016 campaign, he pledged to appoint anti-LGBTQ Justices to the Supreme Court.[^cite_note-589] His administration banned transgender people from serving in the military[^cite_note-590] and attempted to legally redefine gender in order to undermine nondiscrimination protections for transgender, non-binary, and intersex people.[^cite_note-591]

His administration argued before the Supreme Court that Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 does not protect gay or transgender Americans from discrimination (though the Court ultimately decided in favor of LGBTQ rights in this matter).[^cite_note-592][^cite_note-593] His Cabinet rolled back non-discrimination requirements for homeless shelters, allowing them to discriminate against homeless transgender youth.[^cite_note-594] His Education Secretary, [Betsy DeVos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betsy_DeVos), rolled back protections for LGBTQ students.[^cite_note-595] All LGBTQ references were removed from the websites of the White House, Department of State, and Department of Labor minutes after Trump took office.[^cite_note-596] Trump did not allow refugees to enter the country on the basis of their fleeing from LGBTQ-related discrimination.[^cite_note-597] Trump was the first president to speak at the Value Voters Summit hosted by the Family Research Council.[^cite_note-598]

#### First presidency

##### HIV/AIDS policy

In 2017, Trump dissolved the [Office of National AIDS Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_National_AIDS_Policy) (ONAP, founded in 1993) and the [Presidential Advisory Council on HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Advisory_Council_on_HIV/AIDS) (PACHA, founded in 1995). His 2019 budget proposal did not include any funding for two existing programs run under the [Ryan White HIV/AIDS Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryan_White_CARE_Act).

##### Transgender rights

The Trump administration has attacked transgender rights on multiple fronts.

- **Students' bathroom access:** On February 10, 2017, the Department of Justice dropped a defense of transgender students' access to bathrooms. Obama-era guidance had allowed students to use bathrooms corresponding to their gender identity. The right had been challenged by a Texas District Court, and the Department of Justice had previously asked the court to lift its stay, but the Department of Justice (under the new Attorney General [Jeff Sessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Sessions)) withdrew its request.

  [^cite_note-599]

  &#x20;On February 22, 2017, Trump reversed a directive from the Obama administration that allowed [transgender](19904525.md) students who attend public schools to use bathrooms that correspond with their [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity).

  [^cite_note-600]

  &#x20;Education Secretary [Betsy DeVos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betsy_DeVos), questioned before the House Education and Labor Committee on April 10, 2019, about the previous rollback, acknowledged that she had been aware of the effects of the stress of discrimination on transgender youth; these effects include depression, anxiety, lower attendance and grades, and attempted suicide.

  [^cite_note-601]

  &#x20;In May 2019, the Supreme Court declined to hear a challenge to a Pennsylvania school regarding its bathroom policy, suggesting that schools may continue to set their own policies to accommodate transgender students.

  [^cite_note-602]

- **Student athletics:** On May 15, 2020, the Department of Education's [Office for Civil Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_Civil_Rights) wrote a 45-page letter threatening to withhold federal funding from specific school districts in Connecticut and from the [Connecticut Interscholastic Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Connecticut_Association_of_Schools) (CIAC) if they continued to allow transgender girls to compete on girls' teams. The Department of Education claimed that including transgender athletes on girls' teams is a violation of [Title IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX).

  [^cite_note-603]

  &#x20;In September 2020, about \$6 million, spread over two years and delivered by a Federal Magnet Schools Assistance Program Grant, was at stake for Connecticut.

  [^cite_note-604]

- **Military ban:** Trump succeeded in implementing restrictions on transgender military personnel, an idea he first announced via Twitter. On July 26, 2017, Trump tweeted that transgender individuals would not be accepted or allowed to serve "in any capacity" in the U.S. military, citing medical costs and disruption related to transgender service members.

  [^cite_note-605]

  [^cite_note-606]

  &#x20;This announcement took Pentagon officials by surprise.

  [^cite_note-607]

  &#x20;There were about 6,000 transgender military personnel on active duty, according to a 2014 study,

  [^cite_note-608]

  &#x20;and the Trump administration provided no evidence that they pose a problem. Many key military leaders advocated for continuing to support transgender servicemembers. They include "the chiefs of the Army, Navy and Air Force; the commandant of the Marine Corps; and the incoming commandant of the Coast Guard,"

  [^cite_note-609]

  &#x20;as well as retired leaders like Vice Admiral [Donald C. Arthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Arthur), Major General [Gale Pollock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gale_Pollock), and Rear Admiral [Alan M. Steinman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_M._Steinman) (who served as the Surgeon General or equivalent of the Navy, Army, and Coast Guard respectively and who coauthored a Palm Center report in April 2018).

  [^cite_note-610]

  &#x20;On August 25, 2017, Trump directed the [Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pentagon) to stop admitting any new transgender individuals into the military and to stop providing medical treatments for [sex reassignment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_reassignment_surgery), intended to take effect on March 23, 2018.

  [^cite_note-TrumpBan-611]

  &#x20;On August 29, 2017, Secretary of Defense [James Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Mattis) put a freeze on expelling transgender service members who are currently in the military, pending a study by experts within the Departments of Defense and Homeland Security.

  [^cite_note-612]

  &#x20;Federal courts temporarily delayed the implementation of the Trump administration's proposed ban by issuing four injunctions. On November 23, 2018, the day after Thanksgiving, the Trump administration formally requested the U.S. Supreme Court to issue an emergency ruling on whether transgender personnel may continue to serve,

  [^cite_note-613]

  &#x20;and on January 22, 2019, without hearing arguments or explaining its own decision, the Court allowed the Trump administration to move ahead with the ban.

  [^cite_note-614]

  [^cite_note-615]

  &#x20;On March 12, 2019, the Department of Defense released a memorandum with [specifics of the ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_personnel_in_the_United_States_military), essentially allowing existing personnel to continue to serve if they had already come out as transgender prior to the memorandum, but disqualifying anyone who was newly discovered to have a transgender body, identity, or history.

- **Employment:** On October 4, 2017, the Attorney General published a memo considering "discrimination against transgender individuals" in employment and concluding that Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 "does not prohibit discrimination based on gender identity *per se.* This is a conclusion of law, not policy."

  [^cite_note-616]

  &#x20;On August 16, 2019, the Justice Department filed a brief with the U.S. Supreme Court arguing that "Title VII does not prohibit discrimination against transgender persons based on their transgender status," "gender identity," or "disconnect" between biological sex and gender identity. The brief related to a pending case, [Harris Funeral Homes v. EEOC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R.G._&_G.R._Harris_Funeral_Homes_Inc._v._Equal_Employment_Opportunity_Commission).

  [^cite_note-TW-617]

- **Prisoners' rights:** In May 2018, the Trump administration ordered the Bureau of Prisons to house transgender prisoners according to their "biological sex." Treating prisoners as members of the gender with which they identify "would be appropriate only in rare cases." This reverses guidance created by the Obama administration in 2012, and it conflicts with the [Prison Rape Elimination Act of 2003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison_Rape_Elimination_Act_of_2003).

  [^cite_note-618]

  &#x20;In 2018, the Cibola County Correctional Center in New Mexico operated a unit for transgender women; the women were housed together regardless of the reason for their detention. The building served as a federal prison, county jail, Immigration and Customs Enforcement detention, and housing for asylum-seekers.

  [^cite_note-619]

  &#x20;Reporters were granted access for the first time in June 2019; there were 27 inmates at that time.

  [^cite_note-620]

- **Defining gender as sex:** On October 21, 2018, *The New York Times* revealed a [Department of Health and Human Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services) memo that planned to establish a definition of gender based on [sex assignment at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_assignment) across federal agencies, notably the departments of [Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Education), [Justice](52563.md), and [Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Labor), which, along with Health and Human Services, are responsible for enforcing Title IX nondiscrimination statutes. The Justice Department would have to approve any new definition that Health and Human Services might suggest. The memo argued in favor of a definition of gender "on a biological basis that is clear, grounded in science, objective and administrable" and the government's prerogative to [genetically test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_testing) individuals to determine their sex.

  [^cite_note-621]

  &#x20;Over the following days, thousands of protesters gathered in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md);

  [^cite_note-622]

  [^cite_note-623]

  &#x20;[San Diego](28504.md);

  [^cite_note-Hundreds_rally-624]

  &#x20;[Portland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portland,_Maine), Maine;

  [^cite_note-625]

  &#x20;[Minneapolis](6097240.md);

  [^cite_note-626]

  &#x20;[Los Angeles](18110.md);

  [^cite_note-627]

  &#x20;[Milwaukee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee);

  [^cite_note-628]

  &#x20;[Boston](24437894.md);

  [^cite_note-629]

  &#x20;and other cities across the country, and on November 2, nearly 100 lawmakers signed a letter to HHS Secretary [Alex Azar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Azar) asking him not to implement this change.

  [^cite_note-630]

  &#x20;On July 8, 2019, the [State Department](31975.md) created the [Commission on Unalienable Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Unalienable_Rights) to initiate philosophical discussions of human rights that are grounded in the Catholic concept of "[natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law)" rather than modern identities based on gender and sexuality. Most of the twelve members of the commission have a history of anti-LGBTQ comments.

  [^cite_note-631]

- **Healthcare:** Since 2016, the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) has explicitly interpreted the word "sex" in the nondiscrimination provisions of the Affordable Care Act (Section 1557) to recognize and include transgender people, entitling them to the same services to which everyone else is entitled, although a federal court injunction on December 31, 2016, prevented HHS from enforcing its nondiscrimination rule. Under the Trump administration, HHS lawyers began working on permanently reversing the rule,

  [^cite_note-DC-632]

  &#x20;and on May 24, 2019, the proposed reversal was formally announced.

  [^cite_note-633]

  [^cite_note-634]

  &#x20;On October 15, 2019, federal judge [Reed O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_O'Connor) vacated the nondiscrimination rule, saying that it violated the [Religious Freedom Restoration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Freedom_Restoration_Act). His ruling meant that federally-funded healthcare insurers and providers may deny treatment or coverage based on sex, gender identity or termination of pregnancy, even if the services are medically necessary.

  [^cite_note-635]

  &#x20;On November 1, 2019, HHS announced that, effective immediately, recipients of taxpayer-funded grants from HHS are permitted to discriminate on the basis of sexual orientation and gender identity, as it will no longer enforce the 2016 rule known as 81 F.R. 89393.

  [^cite_note-636]

  [^cite_note-637]

  &#x20;This change affects "HIV and STI prevention programs, opioid programs, youth homelessness services, health professional training, substance use recovery programs, and many other life-saving services," according to the National Center for Transgender Equality.

  [^cite_note-638]

  &#x20;In April 2020, HHS acknowledged that the pending rule to replace Section 1557 (which was then under review by the Justice Department) followed the federal court order that "vacated the gender identity provisions" of Section 1557.

  [^cite_note-639]

  &#x20;The replacement rule was revealed on June 12, 2020.

  [^cite_note-640]

  [^cite_note-641]

- **Homelessness:** On May 22, 2019, HUD proposed a new rule

  [^cite_note-RI-84]

  &#x20;to weaken the 2012 Equal Access Rule, an existing federal nondiscrimination protection that requires equal access to housing regardless of sexual orientation or gender identity. (The previous day, [Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development) (HUD) Secretary [Ben Carson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Carson) had told Congress that he had no plans to change this protection.) Under the proposed change, shelters receiving federal funding would be given leeway in "determining sex for admission to any facility" based on factors including the transgender person's "official government documents," the shelter operators' "religious beliefs," and any invented "practical concerns" or concerns about "privacy" or "safety." This could allow shelters to place transgender women in men's housing or to deny transgender people admission altogether. Within the proposed rule, HUD said that the treatment of transgender people would be considered valid as long as the shelter applied its own rules consistently and that this would not conflict with HUD's existing nondiscrimination policy. HUD has been moving in the direction of weakening this rule since 2017 when it withdrew proposals to require emergency shelters to post information about LGBTQ rights and updated its website to remove guidance for serving transgender people.

  [^cite_note-642]

  &#x20;In July 2020, HUD proposed a rule to allow shelters to turn away any women they judged to look physically masculine, examining "factors such as height, the presence (but not the absence) of facial hair, the presence of an Adam's apple, and other physical characteristics which, when considered together, are indicative of a person's biological sex."

  [^cite_note-643]

  [^cite_note-644]

  [^cite_note-645]



On October 27, 2018, hundreds of protesters marched in downtown San Diego to protest the Trump administration's plans to define gender as sex assigned at birth.

[^cite_note-Hundreds_rally-624]

##### Census and other data collection

Early on, the Trump administration interrupted the government's efforts to begin counting LGBTQ-identified people. In March 2017, the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) released its proposed questions for the 2020 census (the census is conducted once every ten years) and the [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey) (conducted annually). For the first time ever, the proposed questions covered topics about sexual orientation and gender identity. However, the questions were immediately retracted. The Census Bureau claimed that the topic had been included "inadvertently" (in fact, it was included because nearly 80 members of Congress had asked for it the previous year). The Census Bureau added: "This topic is not being proposed to Congress for the 2020 Census or American Community Survey. The report has been corrected."[^cite_note-646][^cite_note-647]

Ultimately, questions about same-sex relationships were added back into the census,[^cite_note-648] but this limited approach doesn't offer a way to attribute lesbian, gay, or bisexual identity to those who are not currently in any relationship or who are in a different-sex relationship, nor can it attribute transgender identity to anyone.[^cite_note-649] The same month, the Trump administration released a draft of the annual National Survey of Older Americans Act Participants (NSOAAP), administered by Department of Health and Human Services (HHS). Questions about sexual orientation and gender identity added in 2014 were removed from the 2017 draft.[^cite_note-650] In April 2019, HHS indicated their intention to stop asking foster youth, parents and guardians to self-report sexual orientation to the Adoption and Foster Care Analysis and Reporting System.[^cite_note-DC-632]

##### Employment nondiscrimination

The Trump administration opposed efforts to protect LGBTQ people from employment discrimination. In January 2017, minutes after Trump was inaugurated, LGBTQ-related content was removed from the White House, State Department and Labor Department websites.[^cite_note-LM-651] On March 27, 2017, Trump reversed a directive from the Obama administration (Executive Order 13673, "Fair Pay and Safe Workplaces") that had required companies with large federal contracts to prove their compliance with LGBTQ protections and other labor laws.[^cite_note-Vogue-79] In November 2017, the General Services Administration removed "sexual orientation" and "gender identity" from its Obama-era nondiscrimination guidelines. In March 2018, the Department of Health and Human Services removed lesbian and bisexual resources from its website. In December 2019, the Interior Department removed "sexual orientation" from its nondiscrimination guidelines.[^cite_note-LM-651]

On July 26, 2017, the Trump administration intervened in a private employment lawsuit, [Zarda v. Altitude Express, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarda_v._Altitude_Express,_Inc.) The Department of Justice, taking the opposite side of the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, urged a federal appeals court to rule that the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) does not prohibit job discrimination based on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation).[^cite_note-652][^cite_note-653] The court ruled, however, that it did.[^cite_note-TW-617] On June 15, 2020, the Supreme Court agreed, ruling 6–3 that "an employer who fires an individual merely for being gay or transgender defies the law."[^cite_note-654] On November 30, 2018, Trump signed the [United States-Mexico-Canada trade agreement](58620732.md) which contained a footnote exempting the United States from complying with the agreement's call for an end to "sex-based discrimination".[^cite_note-655] Near the end of Trump's term, on December 7, 2020, the administration finalized a rule allowing faith-based employers to discriminate against LGBTQ employees in their contracts with the federal government.[^cite_note-656][^cite_note-657]

##### Other

A major way the Trump administration enabled discrimination is by providing exemptions to antidiscrimination law on the basis of ["conscience" or "religious freedom.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_freedom_bill)" On December 5, 2017, when asked by a White House reporter if Trump agreed that it would be okay for bakers to put up signs in their business windows saying "We don't bake cakes for gay weddings," as his [solicitor general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solicitor_General_of_the_United_States) had argued before the Supreme Court, White House Press Secretary [Sarah Huckabee Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Huckabee_Sanders) said that the president believes in religious liberty and "that would include that."[^cite_note-658] On January 18, 2018, the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) announced the creation of the Conscience and Religious Freedom Division within its Office for Civil Rights (OCR).[^cite_note-659]

Its purpose was to enforce [federal laws that related to "conscience and religious freedom"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_freedom_bill); that is, to enable individuals and businesses to exempt themselves from obeying nondiscrimination laws. On January 23, 2019, the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) said that Miracle Hill Ministries, a foster care agency in Greenville, S.C., could be exempted from an Obama-era nondiscrimination regulation. Miracle Hill would continue to receive federal funds and was allowed to refuse services to prospective foster parents who are non-Christian or LGBTQ, although it was required to refer the rejected applicants to another agency. HHS cited the [Religious Freedom Restoration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Freedom_Restoration_Act) (RFRA) as a basis for allowing federally funded Christian groups to discriminate against non-Christians.[^cite_note-660][^cite_note-661]

In August 2019, the U.S. Department of Labor, also referencing the RFRA, proposed a new rule to exempt "religious organizations" from obeying nondiscrimination law in their employment practices if they invoke "sincerely held religious tenets and beliefs" as their reason to discriminate.[^cite_note-662] In June 2020, the Justice Department filed a brief with the Supreme Court in support of Catholic Social Services (CSS) of Philadelphia, which sought the right to decline same-sex couples as prospective foster parents within the public foster care system and to refer them to another agency.[^cite_note-663]

##### International relations

On October 3, 2017, the Trump administration voted against a UN resolution to condemn the [death penalty](5902.md) (which condemned the use of that penalty for homosexuality in particular), thus making the United States one of only 13 countries to vote against the resolution (including [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Saudi_Arabia) where the death penalty for gay sex is practiced).[^cite_note-664] However, this was in accordance with longstanding policy, as the Obama administration had also voted against it.[^cite_note-PG-665] Jessica Stern, executive director of the LGBTQ rights group [OutRight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OutRight_Action_International), said the group criticized the Trump administration's "many rights violations, its many abuses of power from LGBTI violations to xenophobia, but this particular instance is not an example of a contraction of support on LGBTI rights... It would be a mistake to interpret its opposition to a death penalty resolution to a change in policy."[^cite_note-PG-665] In September 2020, the Trump administration (along with co-signatures it had gathered from 57 countries) proposed that the United Nations emphasize "religious freedom" in place of LGBTQ rights when discussing "international human rights."[^cite_note-666]

[Richard Grenell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Grenell), the openly gay U.S. Ambassador to Germany, led a single meeting on February 19, 2019, with 11 activists from different European countries; it appeared that no U.S. individuals or groups were invited.[^cite_note-667] The Trump administration claimed that this dinner party represented a new campaign to decriminalize homosexuality worldwide.[^cite_note-668] However, the next day, the president seemed unaware of it. (In the official White House transcript of that interview, Trump asked the reporter to repeat the question, and finally responded, "I don't know, uh, which report you're talking about. We have many reports.")[^cite_note-669] Grenell said the United States did not have a "new policy" but was rather simply making a "new push"; this push consisted of asking for support from European countries in treating U.S. economic aid to other countries as a bargaining chip.[^cite_note-670]

On May 31, 2019, Trump tweeted that Americans should "stand in solidarity with the many LGBT people who live in dozens of countries worldwide that punish, imprison, or even execute" people for their sexual orientation. He referenced his administration's "global campaign to decriminalize homosexuality." It was the only time during his presidency that he tweeted the word "LGBT" (excepting, one year later, a retweet of his press secretary's praise of his "LGBT community" record). It was also the only time he tweeted the word "Pride" in an LGBTQ context.[^cite_note-671] Despite Trump's apparent call for international solidarity, that same week, his administration instructed U.S. embassies not to fly the Pride flag.[^cite_note-672] Additionally, later in 2019, when Zambia sentenced two men to 15 years in prison for having sex, the U.S. ambassador to the country expressed his outrage. The United States did not support his position but instead recalled him from his role.[^cite_note-673]

In early 2020, it was reported that Grenell's consulting firm had been paid over \$100,000 in 2016 to provide [public relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_relations) support to [Viktor Orbán](750582.md)'s government in Hungary (a government widely recognized for its anti-LGBTQ policies),[^cite_note-674] which Grenell had not disclosed under the [Foreign Agents Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Agents_Registration_Act) before working for the Trump administration.[^cite_note-pp-675] When Grenell resigned his ambassadorship on June 1, 2020,[^cite_note-676] he left no one obviously in charge of any pro-LGBTQ "push" or "campaign," and, three months later, a senior advisor at the Council for Global Equality dismissed Grenell's defunct campaign as "a series of self-promoting Twitter photos."[^cite_note-677] In August 2020, Grenell began serving as an advisor for the [American Center for Law & Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Center_for_Law_&_Justice),[^cite_note-678] an organization that has long supported criminalizing homosexuality in African countries.[^cite_note-679][^cite_note-680]

#### Vice President Mike Pence

See also: [Mike Pence § LGBTQ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence#LGBTQ_rights), and [Mike Pence § Religion and LGBTQ rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence#Religion_and_LGBTQ_rights)

[Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) opposed the expansion of LGBTQ rights throughout his political career. In May 2016, as [Indiana](21883857.md) governor, he said that states should dictate which bathroom transgender students may use.[^cite_note-OY-681] He said this in response to direction given by the Obama administration to allow students nationwide to use the bathroom corresponding to the gender with which they identify. A month later, on June 15, 2016, Trump announced Pence as his vice presidential running-mate.[^cite_note-682]

The decision was criticized by LGBTQ advocates, as Pence was known for opposing [same-sex marriage](92656.md) and supporting ["religious freedom laws"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_freedom_bill) that allow individuals and companies to claim religious exemptions from providing services to LGBTQ people, including an Indiana law that he signed while governor.[^cite_note-683][^cite_note-Pence_mate-684] During Trump's presidential campaign, while discussing gay rights with a legal scholar, Trump allegedly joked that Pence "wants to hang them all"; the comment was revealed in October 2017 after Trump and Pence were already in office.[^cite_note-685][^cite_note-686][^cite_note-687][^cite_note-688] Secretary [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton), who ran against Trump in the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), called Pence "the most extreme pick in a generation."[^cite_note-Pence_mate-684] In 1993, Pence published numerous anti-LGBTQ letters in the [Indiana Policy Review Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Policy_Review_Foundation) publication *Indiana Policy Review*, allegedly including one that urged employers to not hire members of the LGBTQ community, claiming they are "promiscuous," carry "extremely high rates of disease," and are "not able bodied."[^cite_note-689][^cite_note-690]

In [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections,_2000), Pence's congressional campaign website stated that Congress should fund the Ryan White Care Act only after an audit confirmed that "organizations that celebrate and encourage the types of behaviors that facilitate the spreading of the HIV virus" would be ineligible for funding, and that "resources should be directed toward those institutions which provide assistance to those seeking to change their sexual behavior."[^cite_note-691][^cite_note-692] The latter comment has been interpreted by some as a statement of support for [conversion therapy](26410.md), an accusation that was not addressed until after Pence's election as vice president, when Pence's spokesperson called the accusation a "mischaracterization."[^cite_note-693] However, conversion therapy was endorsed within the Republican Party platform adopted at the July 2016 convention.[^cite_note-694]

In a 2006 speech, he said that "the deterioration of marriage and family" causes "societal collapse" and that "God's idea" is to prevent same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-OY-681] In 2007, he voted against the Employment Non-Discrimination Act which would have banned discrimination based on sexual orientation.[^cite_note-OY-681] In 2010, he opposed [allowing soldiers to openly identify as gay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell_Repeal_Act_of_2010).[^cite_note-OY-681] Although gay and bisexual men in the United States remain disproportionately affected by HIV, accounting for two-thirds of all new HIV diagnoses in 2016,[^cite_note-695] Pence gave a speech for World AIDS Day 2018 without mentioning LGBTQ people.[^cite_note-696] (The previous year, Trump had given the World AIDS Day speech with the same omission.)

#### Second presidency

Further information: [Persecution of transgender people under the second Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_transgender_people_under_the_second_Trump_administration)

Upon taking office for a second non-consecutive term, Trump signed a [series of executive orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_executive_orders_in_the_second_Trump_presidency), described as a "shock and awe" campaign, that tested the limits of executive authority, with many drawing immediate legal challenges. On January 20, 2025, an [executive order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_14168) was passed by Trump, which saw the U.S. government removing all federal protections for transgender individuals, or recognition of transgender people, with transgender people being completely written out of the law. The U.S. Supreme Court determined that the prohibition imposed by Trump on gender-affirming medical care for transgender minors during his second term was maintained, and that parents were permitted to withdraw their children from classrooms when storybooks containing LGBTQ characters were being read. Additionally, the court affirmed Trump's restriction on transgender individuals serving in the military.[^cite_note-697]

In June 2025, [WorldPride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WorldPride), an international festival advocating for LGBTQ rights, took place in Washington, D.C. Activists from across the globe gathered to express their solidarity with the American community as the nation's far-right administration retracted its backing for LGBTQ rights.[^cite_note-698]

### Joe Biden

On Joe Biden's first day in office, he signed an [executive order](210541.md) banning employment and housing discrimination on the basis of sexual orientation and gender identity.[^cite_note-699] This executive order interprets the United States Supreme Court decision *[Bostock v. Clayton County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostock_v._Clayton_County)* more broadly than the Trump administration had. The executive order also mandates that transgender children be allowed to use the locker rooms and bathrooms of their gender identity, and be allowed to participate in the sport of their gender identity too, and although it does not mandate all schools and states must follow the order, if they were to defy it, the Federal Government could deny funding to said states or schools. Currently, there are multiple states [considering bills](#Trans_athletes_in_sports) which would bar transgender athletes from competing in the sport of their gender identity,[^cite_note-700] and even one state, [Idaho](14607.md), has enacted a bill which bans trans girls from playing in the sport of their gender identity, which is currently the subject of an ongoing court battle in the 9th Circuit (see [*Hecox v. Little*](46676322.md#Hecox_v._Little)). Despite this, the Biden administration has not made any statements on said bills, and whether or not it will deny funding to states that have enacted them, or are considering enacting them.

The court ruling expanded Title VII of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) to ban employment discrimination against LGBTQ employees but did not explicitly ban discrimination outside of employment. The executive order signed by President Biden ordered all federal agencies to review existing regulations and policies that prohibit sex discrimination, and to revise them as necessary to clarify that "sex" includes sexual orientation and gender identity. Also on Biden's first day in office, his press secretary, [Jen Psaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jen_Psaki), announced in a press conference that the President would soon reverse the government's ban on transgender people from serving openly in the military.[^cite_note-701] Biden had originally said that reversing the ban would be an action taken "on day one," but this was delayed, perhaps because his nominee for [Secretary of Defense](43998.md), [Lloyd Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Austin), had not been confirmed yet. This ban was reversed by executive order on January 25, 2021, allowing transgender people to serve in the military again.[^cite_note-702][^cite_note-703]

On March 26, 2021, [Rachel Levine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Levine) became the U.S. assistant secretary for health and "the highest-ranking openly transgender official in U.S. history" (according to the *Washington Post*).[^cite_note-704] On May 10, 2021, the Department of Health and Human Services affirmed that gay and transgender people's access to healthcare was protected under the Affordable Care Act, reversing a Trump administration policy and restoring an Obama administration policy.[^cite_note-Alonso-Zaldivar-89] On June 15, 2022, Biden signed another executive order designed to blunt recent anti-LGBTQ state legislation. The order directed the Department of Health and Human Services to partner with state child welfare agencies to prevent anti-LGBTQ discrimination, identify and address barriers to LGBTQ people receiving federal anti-poverty benefits, and create a Bill of Rights for LGBTQI+ Older Adults; ordered the Department of Housing and Urban Development to identify and address barriers to housing for LGBTQ people; and expanded the federal government's commitment to data collection on sexual orientation and gender identity by creating a coordinating committee to guide such data collection across all federal agencies.[^cite_note-705][^cite_note-706][^cite_note-707] In addition, it would order the federal government to curb federal funding for the practice of [Conversion therapy](26410.md), and ask the [Federal Trade Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Trade_Commission) to consider whether it constitutes an unfair or deceptive act. It would also set up programs to expand access to suicide prevention resources for LGBTQ youth.

## Political support for LGBTQ rights

See also: [Politics of the United States § LGBT political representation](32021.md#LGBT_political_representation)

### Support

The main supporters of LGBTQ rights in the U.S. have generally been political [liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_in_the_United_States) and [libertarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism). Regionally, support for the LGBTQ rights movement has been strongest in the areas of the [Northeast](431669.md) and the [West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States), and in other states with large urban populations. The national [Democratic Party](5043544.md) has held the official platform support most initiatives since 2012 for LGBTQ rights. However, there are some [Republican](32070.md) groups advocating for LGBTQ issues inside the party include the [Log Cabin Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Log_Cabin_Republicans), [GOProud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GOProud), [Young Conservatives for the Freedom To Marry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Conservatives_for_the_Freedom_To_Marry), and [College Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Republicans) of the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania) and [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University). A CNN News poll in 2021 found that 55% of Republicans support same-sex marriage,[^cite_note-708] a percentage that rose to 55% in a Gallup poll conducted during May 2021.[^cite_note-Record-High_70%-709]

In 2013, 52% of Republicans and GOP-leaning independents between the age of 18 and 49 years old supported same-sex marriage in a joint *Washington Post*-ABC News poll.[^cite_note-710] A 2014 Pew Forum Poll showed that American Muslims are more likely than Evangelicals to support same-sex marriage 42% to 28%,[^cite_note-711] a percentage that according to the [Public Religion Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Religion_Research_Institute) in 2018 rose to 51% and 34%.[^cite_note-712] According to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in 2017, [Millennials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennials) and [Generation X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_X), younger white [evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicanism) born after 1964, have grown more supportive in favor same-sex marriage, up to 47%.[^cite_note-people-press.org-713] A 2017 Pew Research Center poll showed that 64% of [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans), 60% of [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans) and 51% of [African Americans](2154.md) support the right for same-sex couples to marry.[^cite_note-714] Religious groups in support of their LGBTQ parishioners and same-sex marriage include the [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)), the [Evangelical Lutheran Church in America](248255.md), the [Metropolitan Community Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Community_Church), the [Union for Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_Reform_Judaism), the [Moravian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moravian_Church), the [Christian Church (Disciples of Christ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church_\(Disciples_of_Christ\)), the [Alliance of Baptists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_of_Baptists), the [Baptist Joint Committee for Religious Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptist_Joint_Committee_for_Religious_Liberty), the [Reformed Church in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_Church_in_America), the [United Church of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Church_of_Christ), the [Presbyterian Church (USA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presbyterian_Church_\(USA\)), and the [United Methodist Church](32304.md).[^cite_note-BJC-715][^cite_note-716]



Students kissing in front of protesters from [Westboro Baptist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westboro_Baptist_Church) at [Oberlin College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oberlin_College) in Ohio

### Opposition

See also: [2020s anti-LGBTQ movement in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020s_anti-LGBTQ_movement_in_the_United_States)

While support for same-sex marriage has increased significantly since the *Obergefell* decision, support has fallen slightly since its peak in 2023.[^cite_note-\:0-717] Democratic and independent support has remained largely stable, while Republican support fell from its peak at 55% in 2021 to 41% in 2025.[^cite_note-\:0-717]

The main opponents of LGBTQ rights in the U.S. have generally been conservative Christians. As of 2024, the majority (62%) of [evangelical Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) oppose same-sex marriage. While evangelical support is the lowest among any religious group, it has increased from 28% in 2014 to 36% in 2024.[^cite_note-718] Arguments against LGBTQ rights are largely based on religious objections. Regionally, [LGBTQ rights opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_opposition) has been strongest in the [South](179553.md) and in other states with a large rural and conservative population, particularly the [Bible Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Belt).

Late in 1979, a [new religious revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Great_Awakening) among [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States) [evangelical Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism_in_the_United_States) and [Roman Catholics](410868.md) ushered in the conservatism politically aligned with the [Christian right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_right) that would reign in the United States during the 1980s,[^cite_note-719][^cite_note-720][^cite_note-721] becoming another obstacle for the progress of the LGBTQ rights movement. During the [HIV/AIDS epidemic of the 1980s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_the_United_States), LGBTQ communities were further [stigmatized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stigma) as they became the focus of [mass hysteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_psychogenic_illness), suffered [isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_isolation) and [marginalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_exclusion), and were targeted with [extreme acts of violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_against_LGBT_people).[^cite_note-722] As the movement for same-sex marriage has developed, many national and/or international organizations have opposed that movement. Those organizations include the [American Family Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Family_Association), the [Christian Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Coalition_of_America), [Family Research Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_Research_Council), [Focus on the Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focus_on_the_Family), [Save Our Children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_Our_Children), [NARTH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Therapeutic_Choice_and_Scientific_Integrity), the [Catholic Church](10380708.md), [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints (LDS Church)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homosexuality_and_the_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints),[^cite_note-723] the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention),[^cite_note-724] [Alliance for Marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_for_Marriage), [Alliance Defending Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliance_Defending_Freedom), [Liberty Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Counsel), and the [National Organization for Marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Organization_for_Marriage).

### Democratic Party

The [Democratic Party](5043544.md) started to support some LGBTQ rights in the 1970s. Despite signing the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act), [Bill Clinton](3356.md) was the first president who openly supported LGBTQ rights; he appointed several openly gay government officials during his administration. In the [2012 national platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Democratic_National_Convention), the Democratic Party supported the repeal of the Defense of Marriage Act and "equal responsibility, benefits, and protections" for same-sex couples;[^cite_note-dem-725] President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) came out in support of same-sex marriage in 2012. The Democratic Party explicitly supports same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-726]

In the Democratic Party's [2016 national platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Democratic_National_Convention), the Democratic Party adopted its most progressive agenda in supporting LGBTQ rights. According to that agenda, "Democrats believe that LGBT rights are human rights and that American foreign policy should advance the ability of all persons to live with dignity, security, and respect, regardless of who they are or who they love."

The agenda is supportive of:

- *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)*

- Passing the [Equality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_\(United_States\)), the comprehensive federal nondiscrimination legislation for LGBTQ Americans in housing, employment, public accommodations, credit, jury service, education, and federal funding

- Including LGBTQ people under sex discrimination laws

- Combating [youth homelessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_among_LGBT_youth_in_the_United_States)

- [Policies to improve school climates for LGBTQ students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_and_the_LGBT_community)

- [LGBTQ elders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_ageing)

- Access to [Transgender health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_health_care)

- Ending [violence against LGBTQ people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence_against_LGBT_people) including the crisis of [anti-transgender violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_bashing)

- Mental health

- "Insuring fair treatment for [LGBTQ veterans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_LGBT_military_and_veteran_organizations), including by proactively reviewing and upgrading discharge records for veterans who were discharged because of their sexual orientation."

The agenda opposes:

- Anti-LGBTQ state laws including anti-transgender legislation

&#x20;In the section on HIV/AIDS:&#x20;

> Democrats believe an AIDS-free generation is within our grasp. But today far too many Americans living with HIV are without access to quality care and too many new infections occur each year. That is why we will implement the National HIV and AIDS Strategy; increase research funding for the National Institutes of Health; cap pharmaceutical expenses for people living with HIV and AIDS; reform HIV criminalization laws; and expand access for harm reduction programs and HIV prevention medications, particularly for the populations most at risk of infection. Abroad, we will continue our commitment to the President's Emergency Plan for AIDS Relief and increase global funding for HIV and AIDS prevention and treatment. Democrats will always protect those living with HIV and AIDS from stigma and discrimination."[^cite_note-727]

[Pete Buttigieg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Buttigieg)'s run for the 2020 Democratic nomination for President made him America's first openly gay Democratic presidential candidate.[^cite_note-728]

### Republican Party

While many in the [Republican Party](32070.md) have become more supportive of same-sex marriage throughout recent years, many are still opposed to the recognition of transgender rights within the United States. More than half of those who identify as Republican believe that same-sex marriage should be legal (55% in a Pew Research survey conducted in May 2021).[^cite_note-Record-High_70%-709]

Republican presidential nominee [Donald Trump](4848272.md) used the acronym "LGBT" at the [2016 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_National_Convention) while acknowledging the recent [shooting at the Pulse nightclub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando_nightclub_shooting). Trump, as president, signed a memo in August 2017 prohibiting transgender individuals from joining the armed services in most cases.[^cite_note-729] Trump appointed the first openly gay member of Cabinet, [Richard Grenell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Grenell). The Republican Party's 2016 platform, which was also used in 2020, opposes:

- *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)* (the U.S. Supreme Court case that legalized same-sex marriage) and same-sex marriage generally.

- Banning [conversion therapy](26410.md) on minors.

- [Transgender children's right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_rights) to use the facilities corresponding to their gender.

- [Covering LGBTQ people under anti-discrimination policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_freedom_bill), including in cases of adoption.

  [^cite_note-730]

  [^cite_note-731]

[Fred Karger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Karger)'s run for the 2012 Republican nomination for President made him America's first openly gay Republican presidential candidate.[^cite_note-732][^cite_note-733]

In the 2020s, Republican-led states across the United States began rolling back some LGBTQ rights, particularly those involving children and transgender rights. These mainly include [bans on gender transitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-gender_movement) for minors, bans on [public performances of drag shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drag_panic), among others.

### Third parties

See also: [Libertarian perspectives on LGBT rights § United States Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_perspectives_on_LGBT_rights#United_States_Libertarian_Party), [Green Party of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_the_United_States), and [Constitution Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Party_\(United_States\))

The [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\)) has endorsed libertarian perspectives on LGBTQ rights by supporting the decriminalization of same-sex sodomy and promoting same-sex marriage since it was created in 1971, while also supporting religious freedom. The Libertarian Party wished to lift the bans on same-sex marriage, but with the ultimate goal of [marriage privatization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_privatization).[^cite_note-734] Multiple sources, including the Libertarian Party, have referred to [John Hospers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hospers), who was in 1972 the first presidential nominee of the newly formed Libertarian Party,[^cite_note-Hamowy-735] as the first openly gay person to run for president of the United States.[^cite_note-Walker-736][^cite_note-737][^cite_note-738] However, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*'s obituary stated that his family "strenuously denied" he was gay.[^cite_note-OGrady-739] [Chase Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_Oliver), who is openly gay,[^cite_note-740] became the Libertarian Party's presidential nominee in 2024.[^cite_note-741]

While many American socialist and [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) political parties initially preferred to ignore the issue, most now support gay rights causes. Socialist groups generally integrate a stronger approach to gender identity issues than mainstream parties. The [Socialist Party U.S.A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Party_USA) nominated an openly gay man, [David McReynolds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_McReynolds), as its first openly gay presidential candidate in 1980; he is America's first gay presidential candidate if not counting [John Hospers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hospers); see above for the controversy regarding Hospers' sexual orientation.[^cite_note-742] [The Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_the_United_States) has been in favor of sweeping LGBTQ rights and protections since the party's inaugural platform in 2000.[^cite_note-743] The more informal coalition of State Green Parties that existed in America from 1983 to 2000 also backed LGBTQ rights. The [Constitution Party (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Party_\(United_States\)) is strongly opposed to LGBTQ freedoms and supports criminal laws against homosexuality and cross-dressing. The party is very conservative and has ties to [Christian Reconstructionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Reconstructionism), a far-right, political movement within conservative Christian churches.

## Public opinion on different LGBTQ rights and issues in the United States

Public opinion regarding different individual LGBTQ rights and issues in the United States is very mixed, with some issues having strong majority public opinion on the Progressive side of the argument, where on others, the American population is more Conservative, see summary table below.

| Same-Sex Couples’ Right to Adopt | **64%** | 26% | 10% | ? | 1,000 American adults | [Ipsos](https://www.ipsos.com/sites/default/files/ct/news/documents/2023-05/Ipsos%20LGBT%2B%20Pride%202023%20Global%20Survey%20Report%20-%20rev.pdf) | Online interviews | February 17, 2023 - March 3, 2023 |
